Hilton Is Growing Faster Than Marriott, Hyatt And IHG

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Hilton opened 182 hotels and 27,000 rooms in the Q3 2026, which works out to 23,000 net additional rooms and net unit growth of 6% from a year prior, according to its earnings release. Net income was $536MM, up from $421MM, and adjusted EBITDA was $1.058bn. Management and franchise fee revenue rose 9.5% which is the company’s core business model.

Comparing that to the other three big loyalty hotel companies, Marriott added roughly 20,000 net rooms and grew 4.6%. IHG reported net system growth of 4.9% after opening 12,900 rooms across 92 hotels. Hyatt posted net rooms growth of 3.8% over the trailing 12 months. The companies define growth a little differently, but Hilton leads on every version I can find, and it did so while adding more net rooms in a quarter than Marriott, the larger of the two companies.

More rooms does not necessarily mean higher earnings. Hyatt’s comparable system-wide RevPAR rose 7.2%, Marriott’s rose 6.5% worldwide and 7.4% in the US and Canada, while Hilton’s rose 5.8%, and IHG’s rose just 5.3%. Hilton ranks third of four on that list which starts to eat into its net rooms added lead. Hyatt’s caveat is its all-inclusive business, where Net Package RevPAR fell 2.5%, which matters because every big hotel brand wants your all-inclusive money right now. This, for me, probably deserves its own post if the drops continue into the fourth quarter compared with YoY.

Net profit confirms the rate growth relative to both peers and net rooms is more tied to RevPAR than absolute growth. Marriott earned $794MM in the quarter and $1.52bn in adjusted EBITDA, well ahead of Hilton. Hyatt’s net income was $58MM, or $122MM adjusted, which is considerably less but HYatt is far smaller (and underperforms.) I wrote last week that Royal Caribbean out-earned Hilton’s whole 2025 in a quarter, and the hotel sector is a reminder that growth and earnings are different races.

Marriott’s development pipeline hit a record of more than 634,000 rooms across over 4,200 properties, with 44% of those rooms under construction. Hilton’s pipeline is 543,100 rooms across 3,869 hotels in 135 countries, with nearly half under construction and more than half outside the United States. IHG’s pipeline is 353,000 rooms, up 3.2% and equal to 33% of its current system. Hyatt’s is about 158,000 rooms, up 12.1%.

Marriott has the biggest pipeline in absolute terms, Hyatt’s is growing fastest of the three that disclosed growth, and Hilton grew 5%. Conversions matter here too. Marriott said conversions (existing independent hotels or those being reflagged) made up over a third of signings and over 40% of openings in the first nine months, and IHG says development splits roughly evenly between new builds and conversions. Hilton opened its first Select by Hilton hotels this quarter, one of several newer brands it is leaning on to fill that pipeline.

As someone whose agency sells hotel rooms, both of these numbers are important but for different reasons. My clients and I prefer to see the rate creep slow or stop (rate regression might be a dream) with more stays. But more properties to choose from is good news too and increases competition.

For travelers, the bigger question is what all this growth does to loyalty programs especially when comparing Hilton vs Marriott. Marriott Bonvoy and Hilton Honors each span well over a million rooms, and their luxury brands include Waldorf Astoria, Conrad, St. Regis, Ritz-Carlton and JW Marriott. But with so many of the new rooms being conversions and limited-service hotels where elite status buys less, that can reduce the shine of the programs. Lounge access and complimentary breakfast (a daily food and beverage credit at most U.S. Hilton hotels) are what business travelers chase but lounges are almost gone completely, and those perks are thinner at a select-service property. Hilton offers elites a fifth night free on standard reward stays and waives resort fees on free nights, and Hilton points pile up quickly through its brand credit cards with American Express. Long term, more hotels should mean more places to redeem, not just more places to earn. But the question also becomes how important those advantages are, and with both Bonvoy’s accelerated status earning, and Hilton’s Diamond via credit card option, the true value of those benefits continue to diminish.

Hilton returned $879MM to shareholders in the quarter, $2.9bn so far this year, and projects about $3.5 billion for 2026. It also moved its stock listing to Nasdaq on November 5, 2026 under the same HLT ticker. IHG has completed $707MM of a $950MM buyback and expects to return over $1.2bn in 2026. Hyatt projects $375MM to $425MM in capital returns. Hotels are on a tear over the last couple of years and Hilton’s growth in room count demonstrates confidence in the market for the future.

Hilton is winning the room race and not the rate race, and Marriott is winning the profit race. I expect Hilton to hold the unit growth lead through 2027, but Marriott’s larger pipeline will make that lead uncomfortable. Hyatt will continue winning on rate while sitting out the growth sprint barring another acquisition which it has been drawn to as of late, which is exactly how a luxury-leaning brand should behave. Still, it’s impressive to see Hilton with such strong numbers for growing the brand.

Massachusetts voters narrowly reject minimum lot size measure

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Massachusetts voters narrowly rejected Question 7, a first of its kind ballot initiative that would have limited local zoning barriers for qualifying single family lots. Supporters say the results highlight voter confusion and potential targets for future zoning reform, including Boston.  AI Summary

Massachusetts voters narrowly rejected a ballot measure last week that would have forced towns and cities to allow single-family homes on smaller home sites than most local zoning rules currently permit.

The vote on the first-of-its-kind ballot initiative nationwide was so close that the Associated Press called the race only on Friday, nearly three days after the election. It was the last ballot question decided because the gap, roughly 32,000 votes, was the smallest margin among the nine questions on the ballot.

Local government organizations fought the measure, arguing it was a top-down approach that limited local control over zoning. Municipalities have made that argument against any reform that includes preemption, winning some fights and losing others.

In a statement after the AP projection, Massachusetts Municipal Association Executive Director and CEO Adam Chapdelaine thanked voters for “recognizing that Question 7 was not an effective or workable approach to creating entry-level homes in the Commonwealth.”

Still, supporters are treating the close result as a win.

“If you told me six years ago that we would have a statewide ballot to change zoning statewide across the entire Commonwealth, and it would barely lose within one percentage point, I would have thought you were crazy,” Jonathan Berk, board chair of six-year-old Abundant Housing Massachusetts, told HousingWire TBD.

The measure would have required “that cities and towns allow single-family homes on residentially zoned lots that meet minimum standards of at least 5,000 square feet in area, at least 50 feet of frontage on a street or public way, and access to public sewer and water services.” In effect, the state law would preempt local zoning rules that require single-family home lots to be larger than 5,000 square feet, which is about 0.12 acres. Cities and towns could still have set reasonable rules on setbacks, height, bulk, and short-term rentals.

Supporters said the change would spur starter-home construction and make ownership more attainable for working- and middle-class families since many localities currently zone for a minimum single-family lot area of one-half to two acres.

While the result disappointed proponents, it gave them valuable information on where to press for smaller lot sizes.

Voters in Boston overwhelmingly supported smaller lot sizes even though the city was exempt from the measure. Rural communities on well water and septic systems were also outside the measure’s reach but voted against it.

Berk said the outcome points to confusion among voters in both groups. Rural voters did not understand that the measure did not apply to them.

“I would bet that a lot of those voters thought they were voting for something that applied to Boston,” he said.

Berk said the city is missing an opportunity, adding that Mayor Michelle Wu has been “a bit gun shy” about large-scale zoning reform. He said Boston’s zoning code is the longest in the country, about four times the length of New York City’s, and that 99% of the city’s residential land is “existing non-conforming.”

Boston is one of the least affordable big cities in the country. HousingWire Intelligence shows median monthly rent at $3,900, on par with San Francisco and New York City. A median home list price of $890,000 puts Boston in competition with California cities.

A knock against Boston is that building in the city is expensive because of zoning code challenges.

“It’s hard to develop here. It should be,” Boston Planning Chief Kairos Shen said during an October inclusionary zoning symposium on housing costs sponsored by the MIT Center for Real Estate.

“You guys want us to lower our standards,” Shen added. “Part of my job is to hold those standards and try to still make them work.”

The forum included representatives from developers The Davis Companies and Oxford Properties.

Policies requiring income-restricted units in market-rate apartment projects have drawn broad criticism. That is one factor in stalling Boston multifamily production. Higher construction costs and interest rates have also played a role.

Rental construction is only part of the picture. The ownership side is where the ballot question and Boston’s zoning code also collide.

“I do hope that this gives some cover to Mayor Wu in Boston to actually pursue some more dramatic zoning reform in the city,” Berk said, citing single-family lot sizes and modest middle housing such as townhomes and duplexes.

Japanese pizza chain apologizes after TikTok AI tool creates unauthorized Dr. STONE ad

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

PIZZA-LA has apologized after an AI-powered TikTok advertising feature generated and published an unauthorized video featuring characters from Dr. STONE.

The Japanese pizza chain did have an official collaboration with the anime, which ran from October 1 through November 1 and included limited pizzas and merchandise featuring newly drawn artwork. However, the video served through PIZZA-LA’s official TikTok account had not been reviewed or approved by the Dr. STONE production committee.

The incident was first noticed after viewers spotted advertisements using character poses, facial expressions and artwork that differed from the official campaign visuals. Questions quickly spread online about whether the video had been created with generative AI and whether the production committee had approved it.

【TVアニメ「Dr.STONE」とのコラボレーションに関する広告配信についてのお詫び】多大なるご心配とご迷惑をおかけしましたことを、心よりお詫び申し上げます。https://t.co/5N2Y05L83S pic.twitter.com/yiLbuaMhhP

PIZZA-LA’s operator, Four Seeds, said the video was published because an AI-powered automatic ad generation and delivery feature had been left enabled when staff set up the TikTok campaign.

The company said it did not fully recognize that the feature was active and completed the advertising setup without disabling it. As a result, TikTok delivered a video related to the Dr. STONE collaboration despite the fact that it had not gone through the production committee’s prior supervision.

PIZZA-LA said the advertisement has been removed and that it has paused all advertising on TikTok while it reviews what happened.

The company also apologized directly to the Dr. STONE production committee and said it would reassess its advertising settings and content-management procedures to prevent a similar incident.

Four Seeds also apologized for the delay in announcing the issue, saying it needed time to confirm the facts. The company emphasized that anime works and characters reflect the efforts of their creators and production staff, and acknowledged that the unauthorized video damaged trust in the collaboration.

Mass Effect 3 modders further expand the trilogy's final mission

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

A popular Mass Effect 3 mod that overhauled the trilogy's final mission to protect Earth is even bigger now. To mark this year's N7 Day, the modding team have buffed out the threequel's divisive conclusion further.

The Take Earth Back mod was first uploaded onto NexusMods for 2021's N7 Day, and originally planned to "address one of the weakest sections of Mass Effect 3 - Priority: Earth." In case you need reminding, that's the section where Shepard and all of the factions that they've won over throughout the entire trilogy come together to hold off the Reapers.

"Bioware's mission was adequate, but it didn't account for the war assets you collected across the trilogy," the mod's description reads. "Take Back Earth primarily focuses on these with an overhaul of the Earth hub to feature more than 28 war assets, as well as cut content restorations, new interactive conversations, new cutscenes and more. Additionally, the Hades gun sequence and beam rum have had minor overhauls, and the mod is built from the ground up to work for vanilla players."

Now, we have Take Earth Back 2. Top of the list of changes - Hackett's speech and the Hammer Landing sequence have been expanded. Before the true battle even begins, the modders have also created a new war asset selection system, so you can properly assign your allies onto a holographic chess board of sorts. Of course, the available assets all depend on your decisions across the trilogy.

If that's not enough, whichever assets you choose will actually show up on the battlefield. So, you might see Salarian's providing covering fire from above or geth soldiers jumping in to help. There's also at least one brand new cutscene and foot soldiers change appearance depending on your choices, too.

That'll do it - time for yet another replay, I think.

The trailer (above) also draws attention to the growing Save Mass Effect movement, which includes a petition that has well over 300,000 signatures and counting. Despite BioWare's demise and Mass Effect Universe's cancelation, this N7 Day inspired lots of former Mass Effect developers to speak about their love of the series.

Biking and Hiking My Way Across Kyrgyzstan With a Fujifilm X-E5

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Biking and Hiking My Way Across Kyrgyzstan With a Fujifilm X-E5

Sweating with labored breath, I push my fully loaded bicycle over Kyrgyzstan’s 12,400-foot Koi Dzolu Pass. The 23,000-foot peaks of the Pamir Mountains loom above while storms dance on either side, pelting me with occasional hail. In moments like this, I deeply appreciate every ounce shaved from my setup to stay mobile.

I am partway through a 25-day expedition traversing the third most mountainous country on Earth. On an unforgiving, remote route like this, you must haul everything: shelter, sleep systems, cold-weather clothing, repair parts, and rations. My primary role is documenting the journey through still photography.

Bikepacking is best described as multi-day bicycle touring taken off-road—navigating dirt tracks, gravel mountain passes, and singletrack trails rather than paved highways. Kyrgyzstan has quickly become a premier proving ground for world-class, high-altitude bikepacking routes.

Weight matters little on flat pavement, but it becomes everything when ascending. In Kyrgyzstan, gradients are often so steep that you end up pushing a heavy rig for miles at high elevation. Too much gear makes the expedition physically impossible; too little leaves you vulnerable to the unforgiving environment. Every piece of equipment must earn its place, especially the camera gear.

Adventure photography in this context is a dynamic hybrid of documentary, landscape, and action photography. There is no staging, no waiting for ideal light, and no stopping the team’s progress to set up a shot. Subjects move from your immediate foreground to a quarter-mile down the valley in seconds. You need immediate physical controls, sufficient dynamic range to save harsh mid-day contrast, and a gear setup that never slows you down.

Conventional wisdom suggests bringing a ruggedized, weather-sealed full-frame body or an ultra-tough Micro Four Thirds setup. Instead, I selected an unconventional combination for a backcountry traverse: a compact street-focused body paired with a motorized video lens.

The X-E5 provides a lightweight footprint — smaller and lighter than many Micro Four Thirds options — without sacrificing sensor resolution or shadow recovery. Its tactile dials allow instant on-the-fly adjustments to shutter speed, aperture, and focus zones.

Changing lenses on dusty, wind-swept high passes is a recipe for sensor contamination. I needed a single, versatile zoom with broad reach. The 18-120mm f/4 power zoom offered superior sharpness and reach compared to standard kit zooms, while staying significantly lighter than full-frame equivalents.

While power zooms are rarely the first choice for fast-paced still photography because of their electronic zoom response, the constant f/4 aperture provided adequate subject separation and allowed low ISO settings under shifting alpine light. And with practice, you learn to work around the zoom speed.

To add variety to the visual narrative, I packed two tiny specialty lenses:

The camera and three-lens setup weighed under three pounds and fit comfortably inside a compact waist pack worn while riding.

Pairing a rangefinder-style street camera with an electronically zoomed video lens might seem ill-suited for a rugged 1,000-kilometer crossing. Prior to the trip, I rigorously tested two bodies and three lenses on domestic shakedown rides to verify performance under load.

Modern camera gear is often pigeonholed into hyper-specific marketing categories. This expedition reinforced that understanding your gear’s nuances and tailoring its controls to your workflow matters far more than adhering to nominal camera genres.

After climbing the elevation equivalent of Mount Everest more than twice over 25 days, carrying a light, highly capable imaging system proved invaluable. Kyrgyzstan’s vast landscapes, nomadic culture, and dramatic passes demand respect — and an agile camera kit ready for every turn in the trail.

Princess Kate Honors Remembrance Sunday With a Pair of Princess Diana’s Pearl Earrings

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

She made an appearance at the Cenotaph with Queen Camilla

When it comes to selecting jewelry, Princess Kate is always intentional. But when it’s Remembrance Sunday, her choices are particularly deliberate.

Taking her place at the Cenotaph alongside Queen Camilla for the United Kingdom’s National Service of Remembrance, the princess was photographed wearing a new bespoke black coat by Catherine Walker and a touching pair of drop pearl earrings.

Originally presented to Princess Diana as a 1981 wedding gift from jeweler Collingwood, the earrings featured a large pear-shaped pearl, capped in diamonds and suspended from an additional stud. The people’s princess wore the set frequently, and after her death in 1997, it was passed onto her eldest son, Prince William. He later gifted them to Kate.

Further accessorizing, the princess paired her new coat—with its structured shoulders and velvet collar—with a wide-brimmed Jane Taylor London hat. She pinned three red poppies to her chest, along with her silver Irish Guards regimental brooch, representing her role as Colonel of the Irish Guards.

Today’s service brought together members of the royal family, politicians, veterans, and military families to honor those who lost their lives in combat. The commemorations officially kicked off last night, with the annual Festival of Remembrance—for which Kate’s look was just as intentional.

Arriving at the Royal Albert Hall on Saturday evening, Kate wore a familiar polka-dot dress last worn at a memorial service for Prince Philip in 2022. She paired the black-and-white midi with diamond earrings that once belonged to her grandmother-in-law, Queen Elizabeth II. The sparkling, statement jewelry featured a floral-shaped cluster of diamonds, from which an additional strand of diamonds, as well as an outer frame of (more) diamonds, hung.

JOTA Cadillac Wins in WEC Finale at Monza, as No. 8 Toyota Takes Drivers’ Title

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Bourdais wins his first Cadillac Hypercar race in his final WEC race, while JOTA wins the finale to extend his yearly win streak at the 6 Hours of Monza.

The No. 38 Hertz Team JOTA Cadillac V-Series.R crew of Jack Aitken, Sebastien Bourdais and Earl Bamber won the final race of the 2026 World Endurance Championship at Monza as the No. 8 Toyota TR010 took the driver’s title Sunday morning.

Neither the Le Mans-winning No. 7 Toyota TR010 nor the No. 8 Championship Toyota finished in the points. Sebastein Buemi, Brendon Hartley and Roy Hirakawa become champions this is Buemi and Harley’s fifth win and Hirakawa, who made his F1 debut last weekend, third.

JOTA’s first win of the WEC season extends their streak of wins in every year that the team has competed in WEC

In his final race with Cadillac, Bourdais grabs his first win under the Detroit badge, and his first WEC win since 2016.

A bittersweet end to his time in WEC as Bourdais voiced his frustration with WEC’s driver classifications that he feels is forcing him into an early retirement with limited Platinum seats.

“Unfortunately, the categorization for guys like me just puts you into retirement," Bourdais said in an interview first published on motorsport.com.

“My window of being a professional race car driver is not going to be extremely long. I'm unfortunately platinum and getting closer to 50 years old. And the platinum side isn't going to change until I'm 55. And even then I'll be gold, so nothing will change. Once I'm 60, I'll be silver, maybe.”

Jack Aiken, who joined JOTA with Bamber while also running the full IMSA schedule and took home the driver’s championship stateside, missed the podium to rush home to hopefully witness the birth of his child.

Cadillac won by 5.607 seconds over the No. 36 Signatech Alpine A424.

After narrowly missing pole position, the No. 19 Genesis GMR-001 took the lead on the first lap, this time over the hometown hero No. 50 Ferrari 499P with Miguel Molina on first stint. Unfortunately, Juncadella was hit with a five-second penalty for cutting a corner, and the No. 38 Cadillac made it to second as Genesis fell to third.

The No. 19 Genesis suffered an electrical shutdown, and the VSC marked the first driver change in the Hypercar field.

The Manthey Porsche 911 GT3 R EVO won the LMGT3 category, while the No. 33 TF Sport Chevrolet Corvette Z06 GT3 R’s Jonny Edgar took the drivers’ title. The Corvette crew of Edgar, Ben Keating, and Nick Catsburg finished third for Edgar’s championship.

Victoria Beaver is a nomadic sports writer who spends her time hopping between race tracks and hippie farms. She’s covered every corner of motorsports that will let her in from 410 Sprints to NASCAR to Supercross. Her daily driver is a 2010 Subaru that she refused to do the smallest amount of preventative maintenance on. Instead, she spends her free time and money building a 42-foot Skoolie to one day travel the country full time.

U2 Sound Revitalized on ‘Carnaval de Luz’

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

In the 1988 documentary Rattle and Hum, B.B. King chats with Bono, then 27. The bluesman reflects on his station in life: “[People] say, ‘There he is, a 62-year-old — and what is he trying to do?'”

It’s a question that U2‘s members may have asked themselves as they worked on Carnaval de Luz, their 16th full-length and first album of original music in nearly a decade. They were teens when the group formed in Dublin half a century ago, in their thirties when their art-damaged rock started to “fuck up the mainstream,” middle-aged when they reinvented themselves with “Beautiful Day,” and old enough to know better when they brute-forced Songs of Innocence onto your iPhone. Their resume boasts multiplatinum Number One hits, instant sellout tours, and household-name status. But now, here they are, sixtysomething years old — and what are they trying to do?

U2 show they’re nowhere close to a finish line on Carnaval de Luz, whose central theme seems to be “it’s not too late”: It’s not too late to party (“Go [Carnaval de Luz]”), not too late to find God (“Midnight Sunshine”), not too late for empathy (“Torn”), not too late to save a relationship (“If the World Is at War We Better Not Be”), not too late to save the world (“Street of Dreams”), and just plain not too late (“Can’t Stop Us Now”).

Each song is tight and punchy, memorable and melodic. The music is the opposite of the blues, as well as U2’s legend-making hits; it’s big, bright pop music with more silver linings than clouds and only a few minor keys. As always, U2 work best when they’re not trying too hard. The songs on Carnaval never recall “Pride (in the Name of Love),” or “One,” or “Stuck in a Moment,” but they do sound like U2 every time Bono opens his mouth and croons something profound (“Nobody is alone but nobody knows that,” on “La Reina”) as much as when he belts something a little corny (“Sing-a-ling the reggaeton,” also on “La Reina”). They sound like U2 whenever the Edge‘s guitar echoes and chimes (“Street of Dreams,” the intro to “The Mirror Maker [Rosalía]”), when Adam Clayton‘s bass stabs and undulates (“La Reina”), and when Larry Mullen Jr. plays a propulsive, march-like rhythm (“Can’t Stop Us Now”) — affectations that come to them naturally. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Carnaval’s best song, “The Greatest Show on Earth,” does resemble The Joshua Tree’s big singles, thanks to an ambient soundscape assisted by that album’s producer, Brian Eno. But it also fits in with the lean, hook-focused essence of the rest of the LP, produced by Jacknife Lee. The band’s last collection of originals, 2017’s Songs of Experience, suffered from a bit too much experience — nine credited producers — and the songs went in too many directions. This time, they kept the production relatively simple and achieved a cohesive feel.

They seem refreshed. Carnaval de Luz is an ambitious endeavor, connecting the previously released EPs Days of Ash and Easter Lily into a conceptual arc. Carnaval, at least the way they spell it, is Brazil’s colorful festival preceding Lent (which good Catholic boys like U2 know means Ash Wednesday, a day of ash) and, later, Easter.

Days of Ash featured the punk rocker “American Obituary,” mourning the death of Renée Good at the hands of an ICE agent, and the powerful, Roy Orbison-like “The Tears of Things,” which obliquely addresses the divide between Gaza and Israel. Easter Lily, the better of the two EPs, featured “Song for Hal,” a classic-sounding U2 song about never feeling alone (“You are not alone when you close your eyes,” sings the Edge) and “In a Life,” which features more traditional U2 drama and sparkling guitar. The final song, “Coexist,” ends with the opening analog-synth notes of Carnaval, starting the cycle over again.

When taken as a whole — and a deluxe edition of Carnaval de Luz does pair the LP with the EPs — the songs present an alternative argument to pop-culture doomsaying, since, as always, U2’s central message is preaching peace, hope, and love. And, well, the music is fun. Right from the word “Go,” the upbeat dance song that kicks off Carnaval, they fuse Nile Rodgers–style disco guitar, Suicide-inspired keys, and hooks galore into a dance-rock anthem with lyrics about how they “came to find other people who feel like this.” Best yet, the song runs under three minutes.  Related Content All the Albums Coming Out in November Hear Glen Hansard’s Posthumously Released Pogues Cover Honoring Shane MacGowan Martin Garrix Set to Release ‘Debut’ Album, 13 Years in the Making U2 Share New Song ‘The Greatest Show on Earth,’ Announce 2027 World Tour

“La Reina” and “Street of Dreams” borrow the spirit of Latin pop without becoming Latin pop (though there is a little hip-hop on “Street of Dreams”), and Bono still aspires to transmit universal truths like “Everyone is on their own, everybody knows that” on the former and “Don’t you give up, and your dreams won’t give up on you” on the latter. On “The Mirror Maker (Rosalía),” Bono may be drawing inspiration from Rosalía, but the Edge is thinking of Pete Townshend as he strums big wind-milling chords on acoustic and electric guitars. Trending Stories U2 Sound Revitalized on 'Carnaval de Luz' Del Casher, Guitarist Who Invented the Wah-Wah Pedal, Dead at 88 The Teenagers Taking a Cancer Drug to Get Taller Good Riddance to the Cybertruck, the Chariot of the Dumbest Gods

The album’s best songs come toward the end, and they’re anything but quiet. The intro the Edge plays on “The Greatest Show on Earth” begins like a Joshua Tree single but then the guitar echoes shift out of phase, similar to Refused’s “The Shape of Punk to Come,” perhaps to show they’re in a new era. Then they settle into a gentle rumination on love, aging, and God. “Is this a church of love or fear?” Bono sings. “Have we made God feel like a stranger here?” Meanwhile, he repeats words for extra hooky oomph on the Martin Garrix co-produced “Glorify,” singing, “They’re looking for you to glorify, glorify.” And “Midnight Sunshine” feels like it could be both a sex jam and an end-of-the-world party song, since Bono’s lyrics — “In the dark, we discover midnight sunshine” — are open to interpretation.

Carnaval ends with “Torn,” a country-ish duet with Dolly Parton in her final recorded vocal, which recalls Johnny Cash’s appearance on the band’s Zooropa. Just like “Sunday Bloody Sunday,” U2’s chronicle of the division wrought by Ireland’s troubles decades earlier, it’s not a “rebel song” but a humanitarian plea. Parton sounds emotional and cracked, and Bono deepens his voice for maximum gravitas as they describe “a family at war.” Neither Parton nor Bono calls out Republicans and Democrats, Catholics and Protestants, or any other ideologically divided people. It’s clear that Parton and U2 just want everyone in the world to try to understand one another on a fundamental level, before it’s too late. That is what U2 are trying to do.

Box Office: ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Thunders with $31.6 Million, Scoring the Biggest Debut Ever for a Live-Action, Foreign Language Film

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The year of unlikely box office smashes continues as “Godzilla Minus Zero” opened to a thunderous $31.6 million, shattering records to become the biggest North American debut for a live-action foreign language film in history. It easily toppled the previous benchmark established in 2002 by the wuxia epic “Hero,” which kicked off with $17.8 million. Globally, “Godzilla Minus Zero’s” haul stands at $63 million.

And it comes on the heels of a string of films that have defied conventional wisdom to resonate with moviegoers. Yes, the box office is still dominated by sequels and comic book movies like “Toy Story 5” and “Spider-Man: Brand New Day,” but there’s been a lot more variety. Audiences have turned out in force for an original sci-fi adventure (“Project Hail Mary,” $684.6 million worldwide); a pair of low-budget horror films from 20-something auteurs with YouTube pedigree (“Obsession,” $524.3 million worldwide; “Backrooms,” $400.6 million worldwide); an adaptation of a Greek epic we were all forced to read in school (“The Odyssey,” $1.8 billion worldwide), and a three-hour documentary about a Silicon Valley cheat (“You Can See Everything,” $73.7 million and counting). Clearly, people are looking for something different. Related Stories Dylan Skolnick Returning as Co-Director of the Cinema Arts Centre Following Protests and Boycotts Eric Bauza’s Final ‘Looney Tunes’ Performance Is ‘Daffy Season,’ Now in Theaters Ahead of ‘The Cat in the Hat’

In the case of “Godzilla Minus Zero,” it helped that the first film, 2023’s “Godzilla Minus One,” was an unexpected sleeper hit, grossing an impressive $57.1 million. But the first “Godzilla” opened to a mere $11.4 million, showing that the audience for the first monster movie grew on home entertainment. “Godzilla Minus Zero” is poised to bypass its predecessor to become the highest-grossing live-action Japanese movie in history. Once again, the film was directed by Takashi Yamazaki, who also worked on the special effects, produced by Japan’s Tojo, and distributed in the U.S. by GKids.

The success of “Godzilla Minus Zero” also demonstrates that subtitles are no barrier to box office success, something that Netflix and other streamers discovered with the popularity of local-language programming like “Squid Game,” “Money Heist,” and “Lupin.” The story at multiplexes is no different. U.S. audiences have filled auditoriums to watch “Parasite” ($53.8 million domestic), “Ne Zha 2” ($23.2 million domestic), and “Demon Slayer: Kimetsu no Yaiba – The Movie: Infinity Castle” ($136.9 million domestic). The outsized grosses for “Godzilla Minus Zero” probably mean we haven’t heard the last of the fire-breathing Kaiju.

The monster movie made quick work of its main competition, Warner Bros. Animation’s “The Cat in the Hat,” which stumbled with $20 million domestically and $30 million globally. The family film cost $92 million to produce and extends a string of misses for Warner Bros., which was recently purchased by Skydance. Insiders say that the studio has lost hundreds of millions of dollars on bombs like “Digger,” “Supergirl,” and “The Bride!” and “The Cat in the Hat” won’t do much to mop up all that red ink.

All The <i><em>Avengers: Doomsday </em></i>Food Collabs You Can Get Your Hands On Right Now

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

My bank account might not survive this movie.

Doctor Doom hasn't even arrived in theaters yet, and Avengers: Doomsday is already taking over just about everything I eat and drink.

I've talked about being a die-hard Marvel fan before, but this is one movie where you can pretty much guarantee I'll be ordering every themed drink, trying every snack, and buying every collectible I can get my hands on. And if some of those purchases happen to find their way into the movie theater with me on December 17, well, I won't tell if you won't. (Sorry, AMC.)

With the film officially opening next month, restaurants and food brands have been busy giving us plenty of ways to spend our money before we've even bought a ticket. So whether you're a fellow Marvel obsessive or just someone who could use an excuse to order more donuts, here's a look at the Avengers: Doomsday food and drink collaborations you can find stateside.

Dunkin's massive Marvel collaboration launched November 4 with six themed drinks, including the Iced Captain'ccino, Doom Cloud Latte, and Wakandan Daydream Refresher, along with special donuts and MUNCHKINS. As if the drinks weren't enough to drain your wallet, collectible tumblers, mugs, cup sleeves, and even a Doctor Doom MUNCHKINS tin are also available to add to your collection. And if you happen to live in New York, Boston, Chicago, or Los Angeles, four Dunkin' restaurants are getting full Marvel makeovers beginning November 17.

Jack in the Box is feeding the entire team with its Doomsday Dipping Box, which packs popcorn chicken, mozzarella sticks, tacos, curly fries, a drink, and three dipping sauces—including the mysterious Assemble Sauce—into one meal. Available since November 5, the meal also comes with one of eight surprise magnets that fit together like puzzle pieces. If you're determined to collect the entire set, I hope you really like curly fries.

Dave & Buster's is committed to the superhero bit, going so far as to temporarily rename itself Doom & Buster's when the nationwide takeover officially begins November 16. It’ll bring Marvel-themed arcade experiences, prizes, collectible Power Cards, and five specialty drinks, including the Panther's Potion and Captain Punch mocktails. There's also DOOMHOUR, when rewards members can earn double tickets on eligible games during surprise one-hour windows, with the promotion running through January 31, 2027.

Considering Robert Downey Jr. co-founded happy coffee and is playing Doctor Doom, this partnership practically wrote itself. The brand has released eight collectible Marvel cans across four drinks: Classic Cold Brew, Tahitian Vanilla Cold Brew, Vanilla Latte, and Chocolatey Chip Latte. Each flavor features two character designs, including Doctor Doom, Thor, Gambit, and Mystique, and they're available online and at select retailers nationwide.

Even the M&M's characters are getting suited up. The candy brand has released special Marvel packages featuring combinations like Red as Deadpool, Blue as Daredevil, and Green as She-Hulk, plus a Walmart-exclusive design starring Yellow dressed as Doctor Doom. There's no new candy flavor involved, but you can enter a sweepstakes for prizes including a Disney Cruise Line vacation, and there's even a Marvel crossover comic book.

No new popcorn flavor here, but Pop Secret is giving several of its existing varieties special Avengers: Doomsday packaging with a pretty convenient offer. If you buy three qualifying Pop Secret products in one purchase and submit your receipt, you can get a Fandango code worth $8 off admission. There's also a sweepstakes offering a trip to New York City and a few other prizes, so if you’re already assembling this many movie snacks, you might as well save a few bucks on the ticket.

Diego Gómez hits another stunner as Brighton beat 10-man Brentford to go second

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Who can stop Brighton’s ­ascension in English and European football? They are setting standards, ­practices ­leaving almost everybody else ­trailing in their wake. A ­second‑place position in the ­Premier League would once have read like a glitch in a ­Football ­Manager ­session. Not in November 2026, where they appear the only team capable of throwing down any sort of challenge to Arsenal.

Under low winter sun, Brentford, an opposition to which Brighton are often, if lazily, compared, were ­swatted aside. If Keith Andrews’s team, and their fans at the Amex ­Stadium, could level blame at a couple of ­refereeing decisions, they received little chance of rescuing anything. Brighton squeezed them, toyed with them.

If Pascal Gross, scorer of a perfectly hit penalty, attempted to dampen expectations of a title challenge, his manager, Fabian Hürzeler, allowed fans to dream. “They should always be excited, it’s our job to make them excited,” he said.

A successful return to the Amex Stadium following five Brighton away trips, more than 5,000 miles on the road including victories at ­Manchester City and Getafe over the past two outings. Brentford returned to a stadium where they have never won in the Premier League. Normal service was duly resumed, any Brighton negatives coming in a failure to add to their first-half goals.

An up-and-under straight from the kick-off was a rare Brentford foray into the Brighton box. Bart Verburggen catching it on the full prefaced an easy afternoon for the Brighton goalkeeper. There are many further dimensions to ­Hürzeler’s team. Last week Paul Scholes, an unlikely ­progressive, declared himself an admirer of a “level of ­coaching” he suggested is ­unsurpassed in ­English football. “The one thing that is non‑negotiable is to always put the team first,” Hürzeler said.

Diego Gómez’s opener was not as spectacular as the halfway-line goal of the season against Crystal Palace but a beauty nonetheless. Caressing the ball first with his left and then with his right, he laid up a thrash past Caoimhín Kelleher. Its trajectory was unerring, unstoppable. Hürzeler revealed: “Yesterday, he changed to shooting with [his] left foot … I couldn’t see him scoring with his left foot. I asked him after where this quality came from.”

The path to a commanding win opened up. Igor Thiago did not last many ­minutes beyond his failed attempt to lob ­Verburggen. Scuffling in midfield with Chema Andrés, his shove was first ruled a yellow by the referee, Michael Oliver. In stepped the video assistant referee, Stuart Attwell: “violent conduct” and “with force” was the verdict from Stockley Park.

Afterwards, Andrews was ­visibly unhappy: “We’ve lost two games and both when down to 10 men. So we need to analyse ourselves first. In both scenarios we can do better. In both scenarios the opposition player has been the one to provoke the situation.”

Infact, both Yehor Yarmolyuk and Vitaly Janelt were dismissed against Liverpool, and if Andrews rues such lapses of discipline he was more unhappy at the initial decision’s video-assisted overturn: “The game is in a state now of a place I don’t particularly like.”

Straying beyond the confines of his technical area in a manner likely to add him to Mikel Arteta’s “massive storage of images” of such breaches, Andrews asked his team to dig deep but Brighton’s dominance of possession became near-total.

Luka Vuskovic, ­serving ­previously as a deep-lying playmaker, won the penalty. Keane Lewis-Potter’s ­panicked response to a physical 18-yard box mismatch was to snatch at the ­Croatian’s shirt. Gross’s penalty was struck as truly as Gómez’s goal. “We need to be cuter,” Andrews said. “I take real pride in the way we defend set pieces and we need to do better because that wasn’t good.”

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Andrews’s half-time ­adjustments, bringing on Yarmolyuk and Nathan Collins, was an attempt to protect Brentford’s goal difference. Brighton continued to pile on pressure, their ­possession share reaching beyond 75%. A set piece became Brentford’s sole route back, though Mathias Jensen’s diagonal into ­Verbruggen’s hands would not have passed muster in rugby’s Nations Championship.

Ironic cheers from the away end came when Brentford won a corner, and then another – rare excitement at Brighton’s defenders being asked to do something other than build an attack. The visitors’ fighting spirit at least sustained, though threatened to spill over. When Kevin Schade shoved ­Vuskovic to the ground by the touchline, another red card was a possibility, only for Oliver and Attwell to rule a yellow as sufficient this time.

Brighton’s push for honours and further horizons will require more than spectacular goals. “You see the Premier League, the margins, you look at the results – we are not ­getting carried away yet,” Gross said. “Every game we have to suffer a lot.”

Hürzeler, like Andrews constantly straying beyond the confines of his technical area, does not countenance any let-ups. When Brighton meet the unstinting demands of their coach, so many are left in their wing mirrors.

Le Fée’s penalty stuns Chelsea and breathes life into defiant Sunderland

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

A lot can change in five‑and‑a‑half months, but as an early winter chill descended on Wearside last week – both meteorologically and ­metaphorically – Sunderland were determined that some things should remain the same. Just as they had beaten Chelsea on the final day of last season to ensure European qualification for the first time in 53 years, they did so again on Sunday to lift themselves off the bottom of the Premier League.

The Sunderland performance was certainly familiar to their head coach, Régis Le Bris – “the identity, the intensity, the togetherness, the way we managed the game” – on the way to three crucial points.

Ultimately a patched-up defence, ­without the injured Dan Ballard and the banned Omar Alderete, kept ­Chelsea at enough distance to allow Robin Roefs a tranquil ­afternoon amid the tumult. Chelsea may be ­developing into a ­chaotic must‑watch under Xabi Alonso but they were ­subdued here and have fallen out of the top four. “We didn’t produce enough to deserve to win,” Alonso said. “We lacked a bit of patience and creativity.”

Margins were paper-thin in a game decided by two first-half penalty calls, the first not awarded to Chelsea after Luke O’Nien’s clumsy challenge on Cole Palmer, deemed after video assistant referee consideration to be fractionally outside the area, and the second given to the hosts by Jarred Gillett after Levi Colwill brought down the ebullient Brian Brobbey; the defender argued, not without justification, that the Dutch striker had a handful of his shirt too but the award stood.

Alonso, as ever, made no excuses, ­focusing not on the officiating and more on how he was “not happy with how we defended that situation”. Enzo Le Fée had been equally unequi­vocal just beyond the half‑hour, sending Mike Penders the wrong way and dispatching into the goalkeeper’s bottom-right corner for the game’s only goal.

Europe may be a blessing for Sunderland but the full-time mood had been bleak after the latest alarming performance in the home defeat against Dinamo Zagreb on Thursday. It had felt like a season watershed and only the captain, Granit Xhaka, Thomas Meunier and Roefs survived the cull here. That the talismanic Xhaka, the subject of strong summer interest from his former coach Alonso, had his afternoon truncated by injury after less than three ­minutes, felt like yet more of the ill fortune dogging Sunderland.

There was no feeling sorry for themselves, though. With this the start of a challenging quartet of fixtures leading into the Tyne‑Wear derby next month, it felt like this match had to be attacked to prevent an early-season hiccup turning into a full mid‑season tangle with relegation trouble. And Chelsea were greeted with determination and ­aggression, with Xhaka’s withdrawal not ­blowing that plan off course. Two zesty ­incisions by Chemsdine Talbi in the opening quarter-hour, in his first ­Premier League start this season, caused Alonso’s men flutters, the first leading to a blocked Trai Hume shot and the second presenting Brobbey with a clear opening that he would have expected to convert.

The passion was there, the precision maybe less so. O’Nien’s challenge on Palmer elicited a penalty appeal that did not end up being costly after the VAR check and Morgan Rogers headed wide from a Marco Palestra cross, but Chelsea could not capitalise on any potential home nerves. If anyone was prone to belated Halloween frights it was the Blues, particularly at the hands of Brobbey, who led the line with enormous presence. He took on and beat the last man Wesley Fofana before steering his shot just too high, past Penders’ near post, and then won the penalty that led to Le Fée’s winner. “He is stoppable,” Alonso said, “but only when you defend better than we did.”

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Such was Chelsea’s bluntness in the first 45 that the coach opted for not just a substitution but a reshuffle at the break, with Estêvão replacing the injured Colwill, beefing up the attack with Moisés Caicedo slotting into the defence. Sunderland continued to fight tooth and nail in a game that threatened occasionally to bubble over. Meunier was booked for a late tackle on Caicedo and shortly afterwards Brobbey was cautioned for wrestling Roméo Lavia to the ground, a continuation of an ongoing feud between the pair. When he was substituted, spent, for the ­enduringly ­popular Wilson Isidor halfway through the second half Chelsea must have breathed a sigh of relief.

Estêvão made one burst through the middle on to a Rogers pass but Reinildo Mandava stepped across to snuff out the opportunity. Yet even with little possession Sunderland looked sharper. A ball broke for Isidor after Chelsea failed to clear Le Fée’s through ball, Penders saving and the rebound not breaking for the Frenchman before Pep Chavarría cleared.

As the last 10 minutes approached Alonso sent on Geovany Quenda for Chavarría, and the young winger finally conjured a goal attempt worthy of the description, fizzing a shot just wide of Roefs’s far post via deflection. Talbi, finishing as he started, pinged one just wide from distance on the break as the home side still looked more likely, even with a third of possession. Sunderland had rewarded their crowd’s vociferous faith.

Kate Middleton, Prince William, King Charles, and Queen Camilla Gather For Remembrance Day

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

This Sunday, November 8, King Charles III, Queen Camilla, Kate Middleton, Prince William, Princess Anne and other members of the royal family came together to observe Remembrance Sunday, one of the most solemn events on the United Kingdom's calendar. The day marks the anniversary of the armistice that ended World War I, signed on “the eleventh hour of the eleventh day of the eleventh month” in 1918.

The focal point of every Remembrance Day is the Cenotaph in Whitehall, the Portland stone monument where tribute is paid to those who fell in the wars and conflicts in which the country has participated. Every year, representatives of the royal family, political leaders, the Armed Forces, and veterans gather there: This year, more than 10,000 marched alongside the king and the prime minister.

True to tradition, Charles III laid a wreath at the memorial on behalf of the nation and led the royal family. At his side was Prince William, Princess Anne, and the Duke of Edinburgh, who also paid their respects with a floral tribute. Queen Camilla and the Princess of Wales, Kate Middleton, watched the ceremony from the balcony of the Foreign Office, as is customary. On the political front, Prime Minister Andy Burnham laid a wreath on his first Remembrance Sunday as head of the government, accompanied by Opposition Leader Kemi Badenoch and other political leaders.

The day before had already been a day of remembrance: the King, the Queen, and the Princes of Wales attended the Festival of Remembrance at the Royal Albert Hall, the major tribute to the armed forces that precedes Remembrance Sunday.

Evanilson ignites eighth straight Bournemouth win at blundering Ipswich

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Marco Rose paid tribute to his Bournemouth side after a hard‑fought 2-1 victory at Ipswich equalled the club’s record of eight consecutive wins.

Evanilson got the ball rolling after five minutes, scoring for a fifth successive Premier League match. With seven goals this season, he has already beaten his tally from the whole of last season.

His latest was a gift from the Ipswich goalkeeper, Christian Walton, who tried and failed to dribble past ­him in the area, and Bournemouth went two up after 27 seconds of the second half when Leif Davis could only deflect Ryan Christie’s strike into his own net.

Lewis Cook tripped Abdoul ­Ouattara in the area to provide the hosts with a lifeline and Jack Clarke scored the penalty with 17 minutes left to set up a grandstand finish at Portman Road.

However, Bournemouth stood firm to make it eight wins in a row and match the record set under Eddie Howe in 2012-13, a season which ended with promotion from League One.

Speaking after a win which came three days after a victorious journey to Sparta Prague, Rose said: “I am pleased with the first 70 minutes because we went two up and I didn’t feel like we struggled or can get any problems, but it’s [the] Premier League and one goal changed the momentum of the game.

“That is why I am much more pleased with the last 20 minutes. We suffered but we did it together.”

A slow start last month at Chelsea proved costly for Bournemouth, and even in last weekend’s 2-0 home win against Leeds the visitors had an effort ruled out after 11 minutes. That made goals at the start of each half particularly sweet for Rose.

“Last week I said we start slow and this week I said we start quite quick and score an early goal. I am a magician!” the German said with a smile. “No, we know we have to be ready in this league. We played Chelsea four weeks ago and we were two down after three minutes. That’s why we learned and always good to score an early goal.

“We were sharp, we were good and that’s what I really appreciate. I said before this week that it’s going to be really demanding for a team that is not used to have this kind of [­European] situation.”

Julio Enciso went close late on with two free‑kicks, but this defeat means Ipswich have only won one of their last seven in all competitions.

Gary O’Neil, their manager, said: “[We are] gutted with the result, firstly, because it’s what we are here for and probably performance-wise not much in it. To lose a football match you’ve not been outclassed in is hard to take. But if you give the ball to the opposition No 9 twice, it’s going to be difficult and that was the story of the game really.

“It’s the level so we have to find a way of not only being competitive in games, but can we now find a way to make sure these Hull [a 2-2 draw] and Bournemouth performances are four points and not one.”

Xabi Alonso must look at HIMSELF, not the officials, as another questionable line-up sees Chelsea fold at Sunderland - and Cole Palmer wastes his chance to respond to Thomas Tuchel snub, writes KIERAN GILL

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published: 11:14 EDT, 8 November 2026 | Updated: 17:34 EDT, 8 November 2026

When searching for why Chelsea were beaten by the side who were sitting bottom of the Premier League, the temptation from Xabi Alonso might be to describe this defeat as a tale of two penalties.

First, Chelsea were denied one when Cole Palmer was sandwiched by Luke O'Nien and Jules Ahoka as he entered the Sunderland area, with the officials concluding the contact occurred outside of the box.

Then, Sunderland were awarded one when Brian Brobbey was brought down by Levi Colwill. The striker had hold of the defender at the same time, and arguably clipped his own heel upon second viewing, but Enzo Le Fee gladly accepted the opportunity to score the game's only goal.

However, it would be wrong to blame this deserved defeat on those two decisions alone. Referee Jarred Gillett and VAR Nick Hopton were weak but not the reason that Chelsea lost.

This was actually a tale of two strikers. Sunderland had one in the brilliant bully that is Brobbey, whereas Chelsea had nobody after Alonso yet again decided against starting Danny Welbeck.

In the three Premier League games played since Joao Pedro's injury – against Tottenham Hotspur, Manchester United and now Sunderland – Alonso has gone with Morgan Rogers as a false nine once and Pedro Neto twice. Welbeck was on the bench in all of those matches.

Sunderland handed Chelsea a brutal reality check as they climbed off the foot of the table

The visitors fell victim to two questionable penalty calls at the Stadium of Light

We are not saying the 35-year-old is the answer to all of Alonso's problems, but he is an actual No9 who scored 13 times in the Premier League for Brighton last season.

Neto was hardly going to have a high success rate when wrestling with centre backs for the long balls which were coming from Colwill and Co. It is a question that needs asking, therefore, especially after another loss.

Why did Chelsea sign Welbeck if not to play when Joao Pedro is injured?

Welbeck came on in the 62nd minute here, but by then, a familiar feeling was washing over Chelsea.

They lost 2-1 here at the very end of last season. Malo Gusto scored an own goal, Wesley Fofana was red carded, and Chelsea, managed by Calum McFarlane in the interim, failed to qualify for any form of European football.

They smacked of an utterly unserious side then, and resembled it here, too.

Chelsea had a week to prepare for this Premier League clash while Sunderland were playing – and losing – to Dinamo Zagreb in the Europa League.

Regis Le Bris' side had not won in the league since August. Yet they were the sharper of the two teams here.

It hardly helped Alonso that Chelsea's attackers never appeared at it individually.

Cole Palmer had a stinker. This was supposed to be an opportunity to show why Thomas Tuchel made a mistake in not naming him in his latest England squad. If anything, he helped make sure those accusations cannot be levelled at Tuchel over this break.

Xabi Alonso tinkered with his line-up and it failed to pay off in a second defeat in three games

Cole Palmer had a stinker in a woeful response to Thomas Tuchel's England snub

Rogers' performance was best summed up by the time he simply sprinted out of play with the ball to gift Sunderland a goal-kick. He and Palmer resembled no-shows.

Sunderland (4-2-3-1): Roefs 6; Hume 7, Mukiele 7, O'Nien 6, Mandava 7; Xhaka 6 (Ahoka 4, 6), Sadiki 7; Meunier 6, Talbi 6 (Rigg 88), Le Fee 6 (Angulo 88); Brobbey 8 (Isidor 67, 6)

Subs (not used): Ellborg, Danso, Methalie, Fofana, Tutierov

Booked: Ahoka, Meunier, Brobbey, O'Nien, Angulo

Chelsea (4-3-3): Penders 6; Palestra 5 (Gusto 62, 5), Fofana 5, Colwill 4 (Estevao 45, 6), Chavarria 5 (Quenda 79); Caicedo 6, Lavia 6, Henderson 6 (Emegha 87); Palmer 4, Neto 5 (Welbeck 62, 5), Rogers 4

Subs (not used): Sharman-Lowe, Barco, Anselmino, Nicoll-Jazuli

Neto looked like a lost little boy trying to lead Chelsea's line in the absence of Joao Pedro.

The half-time statistics said Chelsea had sent more open-play crosses into Sunderland's box in the opening 45 minutes than in their entire games v Fulham, Everton, Tottenham, Manchester United, Bournemouth and Brighton.

Except, they had no striker for Alonso's full backs Marco Palestra or Pep Chavarria to try to find. Just 5ft 8in Neto and the odd other box-crasher.

Trailing 1-0 at half-time here, Alonso made a singular substitution. It was to introduce Estevao for Colwill, who had been tormented by Brobbey.

Le Bris was asked in his post-match press conference if there is another player like his striker in the Premier League. He thought long and hard before answering: 'What do you think? I don't think so. His physicality, his intensity, it's impressive, that's for sure. The build-up is so important to have a target man.'

Alonso chipped in on Brobbey: 'He is not unstoppable. He is stoppable but only when you defend better than we did.' On his own attack, he added: 'We didn't produce enough to deserve to win.'

Sunderland's tactic of hoofing the ball up to Brobbey worked wonders. He scared the daylights out of Chelsea's centre backs. When Fofana first arrived at the Stadium of Light, a Chelsea fan shouted at him as he got off the bus. 'Fofana, please be on the bench,' he said. Fofana may have been wishing he was a substitute as well whenever he was wrestling with Brobbey.

It was the long-ball approach which led to Sunderland winning their penalty. They smashed it towards the top of the pitch and Brobbey bullied Colwill. Sunderland won their spot-kick and, in truth, deserved the lead for the sheer energy they brought to this match.

Alonso is ten Premier League games into his Chelsea career. The honeymoon period is over. Sunderland lost their captain Granit Xhaka to an injury after two minutes here. Chelsea had named a line-up featuring three midfielders. But they never showed any signs of control, and for that, Alonso should be looking at himself and his own side rather than the refereeing team.

The Famous ‘Orange County Choppers’ Compound Just Got a $100K Price Cut—and It’s a Gearhead’s Dream

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

A 38.3-acre ultimate collector’s playground, formerly owned by master mechanic Paul Teutul Sr. of the popular reality TV show "American Choppers," has received a $100K price chop.

Located in Montgomery, NY, the adventure estate has a pond with a waterfall, fenced paddocks, animal run-in sheds, a two-story auto showroom, and a 6,000-square-foot commercial-grade mechanic’s garage and workshop.

Teutul sold the iconic Hudson Valley retreat to the current owners for $1,500,000 in 2019 after originally listing it for $2,895,000 in 2017.

The farm, garage, and family compound, known locally as the “Orange County Choppers House,” would be ideal for an equestrian, auto enthusiast, or investor seeking a legacy compound.

“It’s a substantial property that offers a lifestyle beyond the main house that feels like its own retreat,” said listing agent Danielle Bartula of Howard Hanna | Rand Realty. “The Orange County [Teutul] family had goats, llamas, and horses.”

A gated entrance opens to meadows that lead to the primary log home. The 3,448-square-foot house has a gourmet eat-in kitchen with high-end appliances, custom wood cabinetry, granite countertops, and an island with a prep sink.

The wood-clad interior also includes a two-story great room with vaulted wood ceilings, skylights, a floor-to-ceiling stone fireplace, and French doors that open to a wraparound covered porch.

“This part of Montgomery is known for hiking, mountain biking, and horse farms,” Bartula added. “It’s in the heart of the Hudson Valley with a wide range of everything nearby.”

The main level offers an executive office and a primary suite with a walk-in closet and a spa-inspired bath with a jetted soaking tub, oversized shower, and double vanity.

An upstairs loft that could be used as additional living space overlooks the great room. A partially finished lower level with a bar area awaits a buyer’s finishing touches.

“I could see a family who loves to entertain with family and friends buying this property or an athlete who has to go back and forth from the city,” she said. “It’s only an hour and 20 minutes away to New York City but offers a true retreat setting.”

Other highlights include a mudroom with a dog washing station, a whole-home generator, manicured gardens, and koi ponds.

“The current owners used it as a secondary residence but for the previous owners, it was their primary,” Bartula noted. “I think it would be great to have it used as a primary but would certainly work well either way because it is very private yet very convenient.”

Outdoors, a multi-level heated pool has a slide and wading area. It is surrounded by a sundeck for lounging, plus an outdoor kitchen and shaded pergola for entertaining. The nearby pool house has a tiki bar, full bath, and outdoor shower.

A spectacular heated 7,000-square-foot garage features two guest quarters that could also be used for staff or extended family.

The two-story barn can accommodate up to five horses and has a hay loft and “room for expansion.”

A mechanic’s garage and workshop provides additional flexible space for hobbyists, collectors, or business pursuits.

The 1-acre pond has docks and a grilling patio, ideal for fishing, relaxing, or entertaining.

“There is no way you could rebuild this for that price,” she said. “We have shown it to a lot of people and they are all enamored. It’s a really special property but the hardest part is finding someone who wants to maintain all of this.”

Official | Matthias Ginter commemorates 200 Bundesliga appearances for Freiburg

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

SC Freiburg defender Matthias Ginter is preparing to celebrate his 200th Bundesliga appearance for the Breisgau club in Sunday’s home fixture against Union Berlin. The 32-year-old former German international has remained one of Julian Schuster’s most reliable performers throughout the 2026/27 campaign.

Ginter recently celebrated another impressive milestone. His 420th career Bundesliga appearance, recorded during Freiburg’s 2-1 victory over Augsburg, moved the experienced centre-back into 50th place on the German top flight’s all-time appearances list.

Ginter has started every competitive fixture for Freiburg this season, with only midfielder Maximilian Eggestein similarly exempt from Schuster’s squad rotation. The former Borussia Dortmund and Borussia Mönchengladbach defender remains an indispensable part of Freiburg’s defensive organization.

The upcoming appearance arrives at the end of an especially demanding period for Freiburg, who have endured four consecutive weeks of midweek fixtures. Thursday’s hard-fought 1-0 Conference League victory away at Trabzonspor nevertheless gave Ginter and his teammates plenty of encouragement.

“We’ve now secured victories in all three competitions and are fully back on track after the three defeats beforehand,” Ginter remarked following the triumph in Turkey.It was important to absorb the energy from the stadium and stand up to it. We did that very well, covered an enormous amount of ground, and defended compactly as a team.”

Ginter hopes to mark his 200th Bundesliga appearance for Freiburg with a victory against an opponent that has caused the Breisgau club considerable difficulties in recent seasons. Freiburg have not defeated Union since November 2022. Ginter will be hoping that his team’s renewed momentum can help end that run as he prepares to reach another significant landmark in his lengthy Bundesliga career.

“We’ve struggled at home against Union in recent years,” the defender acknowledged when speaking to Kicker. “We want to be successful once more before the international break.”

Even California’s Biggest Ports Aren’t Betting On Hydrogen For Ships

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

California has been one of hydrogen’s most enthusiastic supporters for two decades. The state has subsidized hydrogen vehicles, backed refueling networks, supported fuel-cell buses and trucks, invested in hydrogen production and kept the technology prominent in its zero-emission transportation policies. Los Angeles and Long Beach, the two enormous ports that anchor America’s Pacific container trade, have been part of that enthusiasm. So when these ports produce a comprehensive assessment of future marine fuels and leave hydrogen out of their detailed deep-sea shipping plans, it deserves attention well beyond California.

The Clean Marine Fuel Feasibility Assessment, released in October, is the product of a substantial examination of shipping fleets, carrier preferences, fuel availability and infrastructure costs. The ports recommend developing commercial methanol bunkering demonstrations, continuing existing ship-to-ship LNG bunkering arrangements and avoiding major new fuel terminals until demand becomes credible. Hydrogen, the molecule that California has spent so much money promoting for transportation, did not make the cut for detailed quantitative deep-sea fuel planning.

This isn’t a state government announcing the end of hydrogen subsidies. It is arguably more revealing. Two commercially important institutions have examined what the ships serving them are likely to use, considered the infrastructure they would have to provide and declined to spend the next decade preparing a major hydrogen bunkering business.

California’s hydrogen story has repeatedly confused technical possibility with commercial demand. Fuel-cell passenger cars proved they could operate, but struggled with expensive fuel, unreliable stations and battery-electric competition. Hydrogen buses and trucks have faced many of the same issues, often compounded by the economics of supplying fuel to fleets. Ports have supported hydrogen demonstrations because eliminating diesel exhaust matters enormously to nearby communities, but a successful demonstration does not establish that the resulting system is the best commercial solution.

Deep-sea shipping adds a particularly difficult set of constraints. A container vessel requires immense amounts of energy over long voyages. Hydrogen’s low volumetric energy density, expensive handling and demanding onboard storage make the fuel system difficult to accommodate economically. The problem extends beyond the ship to production, transportation, storage and bunkering equipment. There is little reason for a port authority to build a hydrogen fuel network when shipping lines are not ordering a commercially significant fleet that would use it.

The ports’ survey reinforces the commercial judgment. Of 38 shipping lines contacted, 19 responded. Nine expressed varying degrees of interest or commitment concerning methanol, but only one did so for hydrogen. That is a survey of carrier intentions, rather than binding fuel contracts, and the researchers examined other evidence as well. Still, it is difficult to turn one interested carrier into a compelling investment case for expensive hydrogen infrastructure.

The contrast with methanol is instructive. Methanol is a liquid under ordinary conditions and fits into a mature global chemical-handling industry. Existing storage and bunkering equipment can be adapted instead of requiring an entirely new cryogenic or high-pressure fuel network. Long Beach is already offering a $1 million incentive for an initial commercial-scale methanol bunkering operation, an approach that can test operational feasibility without committing to an enormous permanent facility.

I have a personal interest in that choice because I had to correct my own shipping-fuel forecast. Last year, I published Mea Culpa: Biomethanol Will Be A Major Shipping Fuel. I had previously given too much weight to vegetable-oil-derived fuels for shipping and insufficient weight to aviation’s competition for those feedstocks. I had also underestimated how far cheaper batteries could displace marine fuel demand. My revised assessment favours electricity for inland and short-sea shipping, with sustainable biomethanol becoming a primary liquid fuel for the remaining deep-sea requirement. The ports’ decision is consistent with preparing infrastructure for that possibility, although it does not establish that their future fuel supplies will be renewable.

The distinction is important because methanol is not inherently a climate solution. It can be manufactured from natural gas, coal, biomass or electricity-based production pathways, and those routes have radically different greenhouse gas consequences. California’s ports can build a technically successful methanol bunkering service without achieving substantial shipping decarbonization if the fuel delivered is fossil-based. That question belongs alongside the commercial case, not behind it.

LNG presents another caution. The ports are accommodating existing LNG-capable ships without recommending major new shoreside LNG infrastructure. It is a conservative capital decision, although fossil LNG’s climate case remains weak when methane leakage and engine slip are included. Reductions in sulfur and particulate emissions are useful, but they are not the same as meaningful lifecycle greenhouse gas reductions.

The larger significance of the study is the change in what gets taken seriously. For years, California’s hydrogen ambitions helped keep the molecule at the centre of transportation discussions. At Los Angeles and Long Beach, the planning process has produced a different hierarchy. Existing demand and affordable infrastructure determine the near-term investment choices. Hydrogen enthusiasm has not produced enough evidence to justify a deep-sea bunkering buildout.

There are weaknesses in the ports’ assessment, including the separation between bunkering readiness and verified emissions reductions, and the question of how electrification will change future fuel demand. Those deserve more scrutiny than a short article can provide. But the decision to leave hydrogen outside detailed deep-sea fuel planning is already consequential. California’s most important maritime gateways have applied a commercial filter that much of the state’s transportation hydrogen strategy has resisted.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines the ports’ fuel research, infrastructure costs, commercial demand estimates and emissions evidence, including where the study’s conclusions are sound and where its assumptions need more scrutiny.

This Grand Touring Coupe Is a Subaru Like No Other

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

It’s a 1993 SVX, and it’s for sale on Bring a Trailer.

Let’s face it, Subaru just isn’t weird anymore. A thoroughly mainstream company, it has enjoyed great success selling all-wheel-drive crossovers with lots of plastic body cladding to people who wear outdoorsy clothes while spending most of their time indoors. Subarus are safe. They are reliable. They are no quirkier than having extra pockets on your Gore-Tex pants.

However, once upon a time, Subaru was goofier than Mickey Mouse’s best pal. See an example of this delightfully odd past on Bring a Trailer (which, like Car and Driver, is part of Hearst Autos): a 1992 Subaru SVX in bright red, complete with low mileage, weirdo side windows, and flat-six power.

See all results for used 1993 Subaru SVX for sale near 94087

On paper, the SVX is a bit of a head scratcher. The design is Italian, done by Giugiaro’s Itadesign, and incorporates swelling curves and a glassy cockpit. The curb weight is considerable, at over 3600 pounds, and the 3.3-liter flat-six engine is unstressed at 230 horsepower. The only transmission offered was a four-speed automatic.

So, this was not some rally-crazed, boosted-to-heck Impreza sedan like a WRX, but a grand tourer. Parked next to various toaster-shaped Foresters in Subaru showrooms of the time, the SVX looked wildly out of place, especially with those inset power side windows.

The strangeness just added character. Further, the SVX proved nearly unparalleled at dispatching miles, no matter the weather conditions. Everything fans like about boxier, more utilitarian regular Subarus was present in terms of unflappable performance in terrible weather conditions. The SVX just added a level of leather-lined polish for a car that turned any sort of driving into effortless swanning.

This example is a limited-run 25th Anniversary edition and has just 16,000 miles on the odometer. Inside are niceties like a power driver’s seat, cruise control, automatic climate control, and a CD player that is hidden behind a faux wood panel. It’s a well-optioned car and would have cost the equivalent of $52,000 when new, adjusted for inflation.

Not all that popular in its day, an SVX makes for an interesting modern collectible today. It’s relaxing to drive, interesting to look at, and you don’t often see them on the road. Should Subaru bring back a modern version? Probably not, but the SVX does give a glimpse at a bygone time when Subaru was both weird and good.

Freddy Adu reflects on his Blackpool stint

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The American trained with the Seasiders in 2014

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After being dubbed the ‘new Pele’ at the tender age of 14, Freddy Adu’s career would end up taking him to some unexpected places.

After becoming the youngest-ever player to appear in United States professional sports when he made his D.C. United debut in 2004, Adu had stints playing in Portugal, France, Greece, Turkey and Brazil over the next decade.

After a stint with Salvador-based Brazilian side Bahia ended, Adu’s next opportunity came at Blackpool, with the then-Championship side taking the American on trial in February 2014.

And while moving from the Brazilian summer to a Blackpool winter would be a culture shock to most, Adu looks back at his time with the Seasiders fondly.

“I had a good time there, it was great!” he tells FourFourTwo. “I was a free agent after Brazil, and Blackpool was cool!”

“I liked it there, I remember I really did enjoy my time there, being around those guys, but the football was really different, compared to what I was used to.”

Adu’s nomadic career to this point had seen him adapt to plenty of new surroundings, but he admits that the rough-and-tumble style of a second-tier English side was something new to him.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

“It was more direct and very physically demanding, so my style of play didn’t exactly mesh with the team,” he admits.

Blackpool would decide against offering Adu a contract, but would allow him to continue training with the club as Adu looked for his next opportunity.

After stints training in Norway and the Netherlands, he would sign with Serbian side FK Jagodina in July 2014, in a move that again didn’t work out for him.

“I went to Serbia and just played the one game, I was only there for about six months,” Adu continues. “At that point in my career, I just needed to try to get somewhere, get some playing time and get my love for soccer back again. It didn’t end up happening over there, I wasn’t happy there.

“They sell you this story but you get there and things are completely different, so I was just trying to bide my time and get out of there.

"Hindsight isn’t 20/20, but there are a lot of decisions I would have made differently, as far as teams go, if I had that opportunity.”

For more than a decade, Joe Mewis has worked in football journalism as a reporter and editor. Mewis has had stints at Mirror Football and LeedsLive among others and worked at FourFourTwo throughout Euro 2024, reporting on the tournament. In addition to his journalist work, Mewis is also the author of four football history books that include times on Leeds United and the England national team. Now working as a digital marketing coordinator at Harrogate Town, too, Mewis counts some of his best career moments as being in the iconic Spygate press conference under Marcelo Bielsa and seeing his beloved Leeds lift the Championship trophy during lockdown.

Legendary sci-fi magazine 'Starlog' returns as a yearly release, just in time for its 50th anniversary

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →
