how human history is remembered in coins, silks, and steles at fondazione prada’s global antiquity

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

ancient trade routes connected distant civilizations through objects, rituals, and visual languages, challenging ideas of cultural boundaries.

Fondazione Prada Milan, in its exhibition Global Antiquity: Cultural Interactions in Afroeurasian Space 600 BCE–1000 CE, opens with an epilogue called ‘An Impossible Encounter’ in the form of three busts: two (possibly) Roman men and one meditating Buddha from the Gandhara region of Pakistan. Although worlds apart, the occidental forms flanking the Buddha hold a trace of the figure at its center. One has an ‘Indian bun’ carved in marble atop his head. Another depicts a Greek philosopher who encounters Indian sages in the first century CE.

At the exhibition, designed by Rem Koolhaas, with Giulio Margheri and the AMO/OMA team, the architect remarked on the textiles, coins, and steles in the show, saying, ‘each and every object has its own demands and comes with its own protocol and its own complexity,’ pointing to the referential and material density of the objects chosen. The collection brought to Milan, which comes from four continents and spans sixteen centuries of Eurasian and North African history, contains traces of what one might define as an ‘other,’ or a world that, to our contemporary eyes, feels extraordinarily far away, both geographically and experientially. Yet in each of the links built along ancient trade routes, these artifacts reveal a persistent human desire to connect across boundaries, cultures, and time periods.

At the entrance to the exhibition, there’s a quote from theorist Édouard Glissant in which he talks about the difference between globality and globalization. In conversation with Hans Ulrich Obrist in 2011, he said that, while globalization concerns a contemporary idea of international relations and extraction, ‘which standardizes and dilutes,’ globality looks more optimistically at how the meeting of cultures, most often those of antiquity, can be a generative event.

The section on migrations opens with a reference to Greek mythology, saying, ‘escorted by a retinue of satyrs and maenads, Dionysus conquests India.’ Tracing the diffusion of wine across Eurasia, the exhibition displays a selection of drinking vessels that look as though they have come off nearly every table and bar in the continental area. It starts with a rhyton, or conical drinking container, fashioned from pottery and found in an Italian necropolis. As you follow the path of cups to Greece, Ukraine, Iranian Azerbaijan, and China, a familiar horn-like shape and animal form appear on each one. It looks as though not just spirits but a band of forest creatures have also accompanied the traders and travelers across the region.

In an excerpt from the exhibition catalogue essay The Memory of Things and the Fabric of Archaeological Discourse, written by historian Salvatore Settis, who curated the event alongside Anna Anguissola and Chiara Ballestrazzi, he says, ‘it is this memory-laden materiality that reveals itself to be ever more necessary today, as we scrutinize the rubble of the world in order to dig up something worth preserving and handing down.’ In Global Antiquity, objects from Roman funerary sites are laid out in all their bejeweled splendor. Found in the tomb belonging to two young women were ivory dolls and gem-bearing jewelry fashioned in a style typical of the East, suggesting that they came from a Syrian community in second-century Rome. There’s also an ornamental headband with stones from Southeast Asia and Sri Lanka, found with another woman buried near Rome. There is a certain tenderness and glamour to these women, one that suggests a worldliness and a value placed on these globe-spanning objects that were deemed important enough to journey with them into the afterlife.

Standing in front of this temporally remarkable gathering of objects, there’s an evident ‘contamination’—an experiential stain that has appeared on each of these objects and challenges our perception of the boundaries between these ancient societies. But what’s fascinating is the second case of ‘contamination’ in the Fondazione Prada space: the one between the collective past that unites these objects and the contemporary moment. It’s in this secondary contamination that a certain inoculation of optimism occurs. It’s a reminder that the world, in its near-inconceivable vastness, is actually quite small.

What’s woefully missing from the exhibition, perhaps in order to focus on the generative effects of intercultural exchange, is an acknowledgment of how wars and conquests also contributed to these meetings and clashes of cultures. Instead, the curators privilege framing these exchanges through the historic trade routes throughout the region. Nevertheless, when reflecting on the inclusion of Glissant’s ideas of globality and globalization, there is a subtle acknowledgment of the potential for conflict and destruction.

In the highlighting of this duality, there’s a sense of duty imparted on the viewer to be cognizant of the ways they move about the world. The evident globality is one side of the proverbial coin. What’s on the other side is something that Glissant warns of in that opening remark: a globalization that ‘reduces communities to a single model, attacking them from the top down, diminishing them.’ This is made even more urgent by the signs within the glass cases that remark on how, despite the institution’s best efforts, it could not manage to bring a selection of objects from Kyiv, Ukraine, to the exhibition.

With all the things that demarcate culture—language, dress, food—there’s a sense that each generation, culture, or empire is its own instantiation of humanity, each with its own parables that get passed on to the future through its material history. We’re in what could be understood as a perpetual state of posthumanism, or a state of humanity that comes after many others. With the fortune of having objects to study, there’s a certain responsibility to continually remind ourselves of the past, not only to avoid making the same errors but also to continue to pursue what makes the human experience rich.

from left to right: Standing Buddha, 2nd century CE / Allegory of Life, known as the “Parable of the Unicorn”, mid-13th century CE / Icon of Saints Barlaam and Josaphat, 1799a view of the migrations section, 5.2 traveling deities: mithra

name: Global Antiquity: Cultural Interactions in Afroeurasian Space 600 BCE–1000 CE

curator: Salvatore Settis, Anna Anguissola, and Chiara Ballestrazzi

designer: Rem Koolhaas, Giulio Margheri, and AMO/OMA

Unfold CEO Ran Cherniavsky on breaking down enterprise data barriers to AI adoption

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

As enterprises accelerate AI adoption, a major obstacle remains: much of the data AI needs is still trapped inside legacy systems, proprietary software, and applications that lack modern interfaces. Traditional integration tools have helped connect enterprise systems, but they often cannot access the full scope of information or capture the business context needed to make that data useful.

For Ran Cherniavsky, CEO and Co-Founder of Unfold, the solution is to rethink how enterprises access their data. Unfold works through the interfaces businesses already use, extracting information and mapping the underlying logic without requiring system replacements or disruptive migrations.

In this Q&A, Cherniavsky discusses why data accessibility is becoming a defining challenge for enterprise AI, how Unfold unlocks information from otherwise closed systems, and what becomes possible when AI agents can finally understand the full picture of an organization’s operations.

Because integration was built for systems, not for AI. It connected system A to system B, one field at a time, through whatever the vendor chose to expose. AI needs access to everything a human being can see in a system, and an understanding of what it means. And it needs it fast, without a project for every field. That’s a different problem.

Companies that can’t put AI on their core data will fall behind the ones that can, and that gap opens fast.

The costs show up long before that. Companies pay specialized consultants, year after year, to map a system’s fields by hand. Analysts pull capped reports manually and stitch them together in Excel. AI pilots stall because the model only sees the slice the API exposes, and gets the answers wrong. In effect, you’re renting access to your own data through someone else’s UI.

And there’s a risk you can’t see: if nobody can read what’s inside a system, nobody can be confident it’s secure either. You’re left hoping.

AI adoption, and it’s not close. That’s where the pressure from the board is, and that’s where locked data costs you right now. You can’t just open Claude and ask, “A dealership owner can’t ask Claude how old the warranty money owed to him is, because that data is stuck behind reports?” Not because you can’t write the question in plain English, but because the data isn’t accessible.

Security and modernization feel the same problem, but differently. Security is a symptom: you can’t secure what you can’t see. Modernization is the long way around. According to Gartner, 45% of core systems still run on mainframes or on-prem client/server, and 34% of organizations now leave core applications as they are because modernizing them is too risky. AI can’t wait for that to change.

Solve access and context for AI, and you’ve solved a big part of the other two along the way.

The same way a person does. Employees don’t work with a system only through its API. They use the UI, reports, exports, files and more. Unfold works through every interface the application exposes, with or without an API.

Our team spent years doing deep software research, integrating with systems regardless of what they exposed. That used to take months per system, by hand. With AI, Unfold becomes the subject-matter expert on a system in days, maps where every piece of data lives and how it moves, and then builds a deterministic read-only collector that gets it out through the system’s own permissions.

That works on mainframes like AS/400, on-prem ERPs, and modern SaaS that only exposes part of its data. If a system has a UI or a command line, we can work with it. Onboarding takes up to 14 days, for any system.

Raw data without meaning is noise. In Oracle’s ERP JD Edwards, tables carry numbers instead of names. A status field holds a code nobody remembers defining. Two systems call the same customer by different names.

Unfold learns each system the way a trained employee would: what each object means, how objects relate, which business rules and exceptions apply, and how people actually use it. Because we see the screen and what sits underneath it, we can tie what a user sees in the UI to where it lives in the data.

That knowledge used to live with SMEs, implementation partners and vendors, and much of it walked out the door. We rebuild it as a catalog of every data object, with its business meaning and its full journey through the system. AI gets the data and the context together, and stops guessing.

Once each system is understood, we connect them. The same customer is KUNNR in SAP and an Account in Salesforce. The same site has a different ID in each ERP. Unfold maps all of it into one canonical enterprise schema, so data from every system arrives normalized and consistent.

Governance is built in from the start. The data stays with the customer, in their own source systems. Lineage comes with it, because we know where every field came from and how it was transformed.

And the collectors are deterministic: the same question returns the same data every time. That’s what you need before you let an agent act on it.

First, we don’t replace data platforms; we feed them. Splunk, Snowflake and Databricks are where data gets searched, analyzed and put to work by AI, and they’re only as good as what reaches them. Today, that’s whatever a source system’s API or export allows. And you don’t have to move the data at all: Unfold can work with it where it lives, in the source systems, or deliver it straight to agents through our connector to Claude, Gemini and others.

Unfold sits upstream. We get the data and the logic out of the systems that never fed those platforms, with the meaning attached, and deliver it wherever the customer needs it: their lake, their warehouse or their agents. At a global retailer, point-of-sale data from an AS/400 mainframe is heading into a Gemini Enterprise fraud-detection agent. At a manufacturer, plant control system data is heading into a CMDB.

Traditional integration starts from a catalog of prebuilt connectors and moves what each vendor exposes, one project at a time. Unfold starts from the system itself: every interface, the full data model and the business context, in days rather than months.

The best use cases start the same way: someone sees data in a screen and can’t get it into the workflow they need. A few examples:

A global retailer needs point-of-sale data from AS/400 systems in more than ten countries feeding its fraud detection, which now runs on Gemini Enterprise. One country’s regulator told them to stop selling until that control was in place.

A top-10 US insurer pays contractors with specialized expertise to map claims-system fields to the database by hand. Then another team of engineers comes in to build the ETL. All of that takes weeks. We do it automatically, in days.

A multi-store auto dealership group had its parts, service and warranty data locked behind reports in its dealership management system.

Now the owner asks, in plain English, “How old is the warranty money owed to us?”

Customers measure value in three ways: time (days instead of months), cost (consultants, professional services and engineering they no longer need), and outcomes that weren’t possible before, like an answer across systems, or a business that keeps selling.

By not touching them. Unfold doesn’t require code changes or a migration. It works with the system as it is, through the interfaces that are already there, and read only, by design.

That matters because modernizing core systems is risky, and everyone knows it. Gartner finds that 34% of organizations now leave core applications as they are for exactly that reason, up from 16% in 2023, and that 40% of the modernizations needed for autonomous business will be late by 2030.

So instead of waiting years for a replacement, you get the data and the business logic out today and put AI to work on top of it. And when you modernize, you already have a full map of what the old system holds and what it means, which is a big part of the migration work.

AI adoption stops crawling and starts running. Today, enterprises work for AI: teams spend months on integrations just to feed it data. Once every system is open and understood, that flips. We work with AI, and AI does the integrations and runs the workflows itself.

Agents stop guessing and start working on the real business. Most agents today reach email, documents and a few SaaS APIs. The core of the business, the orders, claims, inventory, warranty and plant operations, lives elsewhere.

Open that up, and a dealership owner asks one question across parts, service and warranty. A fraud model sees point-of-sale data from every country. A finance team gets on-time-in-full numbers across two ERPs that use different IDs for the same site.

Then it compounds. Agents follow a process end to end, like order-to-cash, across every system it touches, instead of stopping at the edge of one. That’s when AI moves from answering questions to running the business.

It already is. Gartner says that through 2030, the biggest compounding challenge for agentic AI is data access and control. Models keep getting smarter, but more intelligence alone won’t move the enterprise. Models need the enterprise’s proprietary data, and the context of how each system is used.

How to overcome it: start from the business outcome, not from a data project. Pick one AI initiative that’s blocked by a system you can’t get data out of, solve it, and expand system by system on the same layer. Stop treating access as a string of one-off integrations, and don’t wait for modernization to finish.

And treat the knowledge inside your systems as an asset. The people who understood those systems are retiring or gone. Capturing that context now is part of being AI-ready.

History tends to follow the same path. First we invent something. Then we find a commercial way to use it. Only years later does it actually change how we live. The first modern car came in 1886, but it took decades to transform society.

That time went into building the infrastructure around it: roads, gas stations, bridges. AI is at that same point today. We have probably the most powerful engine we’ve ever built, and the models keep getting better. But the transformation inside enterprises hasn’t happened yet, and the reason isn’t intelligence. The roads aren’t there.

Integration is a big part of those roads, and it was built for a different kind of traffic. It connected systems to systems with connectors and pipes, moving one field at a time. Over the next three to five years, the traffic changes. AI is now the one that needs the data, and AI needs all of it, along with what it means.

So I think the market splits. Moving data becomes a commodity, like a basic road. The value moves to the layer that understands the enterprise: full access to every system, the business context behind it, and one schema across all of them.

Gartner already treats AI context platforms as a separate market, growing from $28B in 2026 to $78B by 2030. It also notes that these platforms are limited by how much they can actually get out of systems of record. That’s exactly the gap we fill.

The difference this time is that we don’t have to wait 40 years. With AI, enterprises can build the roads much faster than they built them for the car, but only if their data can actually reach the engine. Our goal is to lead this category and become the knowledge foundation for enterprise AI, so that every AI use case in the enterprise runs on Unfold, from the mainframe to SaaS.

Our team spent years doing deep software research, getting data out of systems that were never designed to give it up. Then AI arrived, and suddenly every company had the same problem we’d been solving by hand, at a scale nobody could staff. Twenty years ago, your data sat on your own servers.

Today it sits behind vendors, interfaces, and knowledge that’s retiring. We built Unfold so AI can understand any enterprise system the way a long-time employee does. The long-term vision is simple: every AI use case in the enterprise runs on Unfold, from the mainframe to SaaS.

Dogecoin Cofounder Highlights Shiba Inu Popularity in Japan

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Dogecoin cofounder Billy Markus shared a lighthearted observation about Japan's unique dog culture in a post on X, pointing out the popularity of Shiba Inu dogs in Japan.

"Like, 50% of the dogs I see in Japan are Shiba Inu," wrote Markus, who goes by the online handle Shibetoshi Nakamoto.

like 50% of the dogs i see in japan are shiba inu i love japan

The Dogecoin cofounder's comment is based on his own observation when he was in Japan, where the Shiba Inu breed has deep cultural roots and is still one of the country's most recognizable native dog breeds. Markus' observation is noteworthy, as he is the cofounder of Dogecoin, a cryptocurrency that draws its origins from the Shiba Inu dog.

On December 6, 2013, Dogecoin was launched, taking influence from the famous Doge meme: an image of a very adorable Shiba Inu dog with multi-colored Comic Sans text that appears as the dog's "thought bubbles."

This famous meme was based on a 2010 photograph of a Japanese female Shiba Inu dog, Kabosu, adopted by Japanese kindergarten teacher Atsuko Satō from an animal shelter in 2008. This helped establish the dog-themed cryptocurrency trend that later inspired several other tokens, including Shiba Inu (SHIB).

Kabosu, a Japanese Shiba Inu, inspired a generation of online jokes and became the face of Dogecoin cryptocurrency. Kabosu was the face of the breezy Doge meme, her quizzical expression surrounded by an inner monologue of choppy phrases in bad grammar written in rainbow-colored Comic Sans font.

In 2010, two years after adopting Kabosu from a puppy mill where she would otherwise have been put down, Ms. Satō, a teacher from Sakura, east of Tokyo, took a picture of her pet crossing her paws on the sofa. She posted the image on her blog, from where it spread to online forum Reddit and became a meme that bounced from college bedrooms to office email chains.

This inspired Dogecoin, which was started as a joke by two software engineers, Billy Markus and Jackson Palmer, and is now the 12th largest cryptocurrency, with a market capitalization of $11.17 billion, according to CoinMarketCap data.

Kabosu, the dog behind the "doge" meme, died in May 2024 after 14 years of internet fame.

SHT Days 21, 22: Wrapping Up 2026

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Today was one of the most delightful days I’ve ever had on any trail! My husband decided to skip hiking with me today and opted to catch up on some emails. There was supposed to be weather rolling in later anyway, so I could just put my head down and fly. But that’s not the reason today was delightful. Let me tell you why.

We got an early start today to beat the rain that is due this afternoon. Bill dropped me back off at that spot on Old Ski Hill Road. There’s not technically a trailhead here, and the area seems country-residential, but there is a wide spot on the gravel road near where the trail crosses that one could park briefly. I continued SOBO down easy snowmobile track for the first few miles until the trail crossed Cook County Road 6. I found Bill in his truck here, parked on the side of the road, waiting for me with a hot latte and a chocolate croissant! He had run into Grand Marais and back out to this trail crossing. How sweet!

I enjoyed my jolt of caffeine and sugar and continued on. The trail travels through the boggy and wet Sundling Creek area on a long section of raised boardwalk here. What a chore that must have been for the trail builders when they did this! I admired their work and took a few pictures before and after I crossed, and was thankful the rain hadn’t started yet. I imagined the horror of slipping on the wet boards and falling into the flooded pond entirely.

Crossing the flooded Sundling Creek on a long boardwalk.

I passed the Bally Creek Pond Campsites just in time, needing to complete at least one of my L&L’s (courtesy of aforementioned latte) before crossing Bally Creek Road and trailhead. Who do I find here? None of than Bill again, of course! This time it was a doozy. He had brought along our small air fryer from the camper and cooked up a few frozen burritos for lunch! What a swell guy! At this point a light rain had begun to fall, so it was nice to sit inside a dry vehicle and enjoy my grub in the warmth.

A heavy mist blanketed the landscape this afternoon.

I could have called it a day here, but I really wanted to make tomorrow, my last planned hiking day, short and easy. With the amount of rain forecast for tonight, I was worried about the state of the trail as well. So, I said goodbye to Bill once again and marched on.

The next five and a half miles can only be described as a very pleasant forest walk. The terrain was pretty nice, and the inclines and declines were gentle. There was another random section of big, beautiful pines. I just love walking through these giants. It smells good, pine needles soften the trail, and right at the moment they were sheltering me from a light rain.

Approaching Cty Rd 45 and a glimpse of the river we will be following tomorrow.

I eventually reached the North Cascade River Campsite and signed the trail register here. The precipitation was already beginning to become more than a light sprinkle as I followed the Cascade River down the last mile out of 12 total to Cook County Road 45 where I met Bill for the final time. He had a thermos of hot vegetable beef soup ready to warm me back up. I could get used to this! I had my partner and best friend meet me at every trail crossing today with a smile and a treat. That, my friends, is how today was made exceptionally delightful!

Last night it rained cats and dogs. I mean, it just kept downpouring for hours and hours. We have just a small teardrop camper, without a bathroom, and the galley is outside. Even though we had a nice tarp system set up, the rain was finding a way to seep through and soak everything.

When we came back from the trail yesterday, we sheltered inside the camper for a few hours but we started to get hungry for supper. There was definitely no way we were going to venture outside to scrounge up something to eat, so we decided to splurge on town food. We quickly hopped in the truck and drove just down the road to the Cascade Lodge Restaurant for dinner. The place was packed to the brim. Everyone else had the same idea again to escape the weather.

This morning we slept in. We knew it would be a wet world once we clambered out of our small sleeping space, so we were avoiding it, at least until it warmed up a little. Eventually, we opened the hatch to be greeted with sunshine, albeit cold and wet. We had a quick breakfast and made our way back to Cook County Road 45.

Bill was going to finish this out with me today. We had about 4 miles left to do, and we were hoping for nice scenery alongside the Cascade River. There was a short uphill road walk upon leaving the trailhead before it entered the woods on single track. It was a cold and wet start to the day, but the sunshine was promising.

The natural curve of this tree made for a perfect break spot!

The trail was mostly gentle for the first couple of miles, except where it crossed a few streams where campsites Cut Log and Boreal had been placed. The poor campers who chose either of these spots would have very steep jaunts from camp to filter water.

The first of many waterfalls and cascades we will see today.

Shortly after passing the Boreal campsite the trail hugs the Cascade River. The remaining trail back to our campsite was stunning. The Cascade River is absolutely beautiful, constantly showing off gorgeous waterfalls, and well, cascades. We couldn’t take enough pictures. What a way to finish the trail this year! It was a beautiful sunny day, hiking along what has to be one of the most amazing sections of the Superior Hiking Trail.

As we neared the end of the river where it spills into Lake Superior, we hopped onto a short spur trail that leads back into Cascade River State Park. The trail re-entered the park practically right at our campsite. It was an incredibly convenient end to not only today’s hike, but my 2026 SHT adventure. The SHT handed me everything I could expect from a backpacking journey-wildly varying weather, challenging terrain, and rewarding views. I feel proud of what I accomplished this year, and had a lot of fun doing it. As Bill and I packed up our campsite to head home, I feel myself already excited to return for the remaining 80 miles.

This website contains affiliate links, which means The Trek may receive a percentage of any product or service you purchase using the links in the articles or advertisements. The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

To learn more, please visit the About This Site page.

My name is Misty, and I hail from Nebraska, where I live with my husband, two cats, and flock of chickens. I worked in healthcare for over 16 years before giving it up for more outdoor pursuits. I hope to have you follow along on my new adventures!

Notify me via e-mail if anyone answers my comment.

Is Frontier's Business Bundle worth the extra miles? I put it to the test

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Can a low-cost carrier ever really be premium? Frontier Airlines certainly seems to think so.

In recent years, the airline has added front-of-plane seating with extra legroom and blocked middle seats, combined its extras into fare bundles and even announced plans for first class.

You can pay for those bundles with Frontier Miles, too. The top-tier Business Bundle is the closest thing Frontier has to a premium experience, with just about every bell and whistle the airline offers.

The catch? It usually costs more than double the miles of a bare-bones Basic fare.

Are the extra miles worth it? I put it to the test by taking one flight on a Basic fare, then another on the Business Bundle.

Related: Frontier wants to be an airline you'd choose to fly. Will first class and new perks do the trick?

Frontier sells a no-frills Basic fare plus three bundles that add bags, seats and flexibility, and you can book any of them with cash or miles.

The amounts below are minimum one-way prices per traveler. Taxes and fees start at $5.60, and miles can only be redeemed for a bundle at the time of the original booking.

Related: Flying Frontier Airlines? You can now redeem miles for seats, bags and other perks

My first Frontier flight was from St. Louis Lambert International Airport (STL) to Denver International Airport (DEN). I paid for it with the 5,000 miles I'd earned from Frontier Airlines' Super Bowl promotion earlier this year (offer no longer available), so only the $5.60 in taxes and fees came out of my pocket. Transferring Rove miles to Frontier is also an easy way to top up your balance.

You really can't argue with that value for a two-hour flight. The trade-off, as anyone who's flown a low-cost carrier will know, is everything you don't get.

A Basic fare only covers a personal item. That turned out to be the biggest frustration of my Frontier experience, as this won't be a realistic option for folks traveling for longer than a day or two.

Boarding in Group 5 as a Frontier Miles member wasn't an issue, and the process was more orderly and civilized than I expected. With nothing to put in the overhead bins, there wasn't a need to board early anyway.

I just found my seat all the way back in row 27 (I couldn't select it myself) and settled in.

The flight itself was uneventful and punctual, which on a low-cost airline is really about all you can ask for.

Related: How to use credit cards to defeat basic economy

For my second flight, a short hop from Denver to Kansas City International Airport (MCI), I booked the Business Bundle, and the perks landed before I even got to the airport.

Being able to select Seat 1A during booking sure was convenient. True to the UpFront Plus promise, the seat next to me was empty.

Frontier's new 2-2 first-class seats had not yet entered service when I flew, so my aircraft retained the existing 3-3 layout.

I boarded in Group 1, right behind preboarding, and didn't need to pay extra for luggage. The bundle included a full-size carry-on and two checked bags, each weighing up to 50 pounds, which was far more than I needed for this short trip.

What you won't get, in any bundle, is complimentary food or drinks.

Frontier doesn't include food, drinks or lounge access with its bundles, so anything you want on board comes at an extra cost, and it was a sobering reminder that this was still a low-cost carrier.

Related: Frontier makes it easier to earn top-tier elite status in 2027 but cuts earning rates for basic fares and noncardholders

Unless I needed to pack a lot of luggage or particularly valued the extra space of an UpFront Plus seat, I don't think I'd spring for a Business Bundle again, whether I was paying with cash or redeeming Frontier miles.

For even the cheapest redemptions, the Business Bundle costs 8,000 more miles than a Basic fare, which is more than double the price. For a short domestic flight with nothing to eat or drink, that's a lot more miles.

Frontier's Business Bundle is not a bad product. It's just that a Basic fare for as little as 5,000 miles at that price is terrific value, and that's a tough act to follow.

For most travelers, the sweet spot is probably the Economy Bundle. It starts at just 2,000 more miles than a Basic fare and fixes the thing I missed most on my first flight, a proper carry-on, while making your booking far more flexible.

I'd be happy with that for my next Frontier flight.

Related: Frontier Airlines' planes set for big upgrade: Starlink Wi-Fi coming in 2027

So, can a low-cost carrier be premium? After flying both fares back-to-back, the Business Bundle felt more like paying for convenience, baggage and personal space than a genuinely premium experience.

Your own experience will depend a lot on the route, the dates and how much luggage you need to take, and Frontier's lowest award rates won't always be available.

Unless you need two checked bags or place a high value on the extra space of an UpFront Plus seat, the Economy Bundle is probably the better balance of price and useful benefits.

Bringing Back the Image Formats Apple Forgot

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Bringing Back the Image Formats Apple Forgot

The 1990s brought Bill Clinton, The Simpsons, MP3s, Napster – and the age of personal digital photography and personal computer video.

The revolution began with digital cameras and Apple’s 1994 launch of the QuickTake 100. The path was pixelated at first, with heavily compressed, low-resolution photos that couldn’t begin to replace film. Still, the fuzzy handwriting was on the wall: the future was filmless.

As digital cameras were beginning to walk upright, a parallel evolution was taking place: personal computers gained the ability to play video clips, thanks to Apple’s QuickTime and, later, Microsoft’s Video for Windows. The quality was poor – grainy images and jerky motion within a saltine-sized frame. But it was sort-of TV…on a computer!

In the middle of these developments, Apple created an imaging technology that had no analog in the world of, well, analog. Introduced in 1995, QuickTime VR enabled photographers to produce a 360-degree panorama that appeared on a computer screen and that you could navigate with your mouse and keyboard.

This wasn’t the kind of VR we associate with today’s VR headsets – a 1995 Apple tech note called it “virtual reality for the rest of us.” You changed your vantage point by dragging within the panorama. Today, of course, this kind of panning is common (think Google Street View). But in the 1990s, it felt like something completely new, because it was.

Within ten years, it was all but abandoned. As Apple evolved the Mac’s operating system, the company left the QuickTime VR and QuickTake formats behind. This is the story of my efforts to bring them back.

I was a contributing editor and columnist for Macworld magazine during this era and was lucky to be able to cover digital cameras and QuickTime VR. I wrote a feature article on digital cameras in 1994 (PDF, 1.8MB) and led the magazine’s coverage of QuickTime VR in 1995 (PDF, 1.8MB). For a Macworld-branded book I wrote, I produced a CD-ROM containing an interview with the QuickTake 150 product manager.

In early 1995, I spent time with a team in Apple’s Advanced Technology Group, learning about QuickTime VR and what was initially a painful production process. It involved film! You’d mount a 35mm camera on a special tripod bracket, then shoot a series of overlapping images, then have the film processed, scanned, and transferred to Kodak PhotoCD discs. Finally, you’d stitch the images together with a $2000 software suite that required Apple’s MPW programming tool and lots of arcane typed commands.

Reminder: We shoot panos with our phones now.

Primitive as it was, QuickTime VR enchanted the amateur photographer in me. I’d been a technology journalist since the early 1980s, but computers and photography lived in disconnected circles of my life’s Venn diagram. In the 90s, those circles intersected and my job got a lot more fun.

In 1997, I took a QuickTake camera on a seven-week, 26-state road trip, shooting QuickTake photos and producing QuickTime VR movies along the way.

Between 1997 and 2001, I participated in a series of worldwide QuickTime VR celebrations called A Wrinkle in Time. Four times a year, dozens of QuickTime VR photographers around the world shot panoramas at the moment of the equinoxes and solstices.

Our work was published on the web, and visitors could revel in virtual views of each participant’s world – some shot at night, others by day, some in snow, others in Hawaii, but all taken at the same time. The project made the world feel smaller; it was a beautiful example of the promise of the web, where everyone had a voice that wasn’t drowned out by social networks and their ads and algorithms.

QuickTime VR was embraced by content creators of all kinds. Publishers produced commercial CD-ROMs whose panoramas enabled virtual travel – to other countries, to lost Mayan cities, to Star Trek’s USS Enterprise. On the web, museums and universities created virtual exhibits showcasing exotic places such as the pyramids of Giza and exotic objects such as primitive hearing aids. Architects and real estate agents created walkthroughs. Photographers took us to wilderness parks and Yosemite National Park. NBC News even published crime scene panoramas during the O.J. Simpson trial. And photographers, pro and amateur alike, explored this new way to capture the world around them.

Then Steve Jobs returned to Apple and everything began to change.

The Apple of 1997 had a broad product line that included multiple Mac models, numerous software initiatives (including QuickTime VR), and a family of peripherals – hard drives, printers, a scanner, and the QuickTake camera.

When Steve Jobs returned to Apple in 1997, he pruned that tree with a hatchet. Jobs cut 70 percent of the product line, chopping it down to four cornerstones: iMac, iBook, PowerBook, and Power Mac.

QuickTake cameras didn’t fit those quadrants. Apple introduced the QuickTake 200 in early 1997; by the end of that year, it had discontinued its entire camera line.

Over time, photos taken with the QuickTake 100 and 150 ended up in the file-format dustbin. Mac OS X couldn’t open them – you had to use the Classic environment, that stopgap compatibility layer that enabled Mac OS X to run the previous operating system.

When Classic went away in 2007, thousands – maybe millions – of photos became unreadable on anything other than an older computer. You could convert those photos using specialized utilities like Graphic Converter, but you couldn’t just open them in their native form.

QuickTime VR had a similar slow slide into obsolescence. Apple shipped its QuickTime VR Authoring Studio – an elegant software package for QTVR production – in November 1997. But the Advanced Technology Group where QTVR originated had already been shuttered a month prior.

Apple never developed an OS X version of Authoring Studio; QuickTime VR producers had to run it under the Classic environment. When Classic went away in 2007, it was either “use an old Mac” or switch to a third-party authoring tool.

By then, though, there was little reason. QuickTime VR was on its way to obscurity. The web killed the CD-ROM industry as publishers abandoned those silver platters in search of internet goldmines.

And the web itself changed. In the web’s early days, displaying rich-media content such as video required website visitors to download browser plug-ins. Messages like This website requires QuickTime or Download the Flash plug-in were common. This complicated life for web visitors and web designers alike.

Over time, the web community developed HTML standards that eliminated plug-ins. It was the right move – but it removed the ability to display QuickTime VR movies on a web page.

Apple continued to support QuickTime VR movies in its standalone QuickTime Player app, though even that viewing option wasn’t exactly obvious. You had to manually install QuickTime Player 7, which you either had to download or unearth from the macOS installation CD.

Then came 2019’s macOS Catalina. That version abandoned support for the 32-bit architecture of earlier Mac operating systems, and QuickTime Player 7 wouldn’t run. After that, attempting to open a QuickTime VR movie earned you an error message.

As for the web, many thousands of QuickTime VR movies were published during the format’s lifetime. Many still survive on old cobweb sites and on the Internet Archive. But they can’t be viewed with modern browsers or Apple’s current QuickTime Player.

Apple didn’t have to forsake these formats. It could have continued supporting the QuickTake photo format, and it could have adapted its QuickTime VR Authoring Studio software to run in Mac OS X and to export web panoramas that didn’t require browser plug-ins.

But business is business. Maintaining software isn’t free, and when the potential market is relatively small, spreadsheets are consulted and products die.

And so do those memories captured by the original QuickTake cameras. Does it matter? After all, the drives that held those photos can’t even be connected to a Mac made in this century. (Which illustrates the twin-bladed sword of digital preservation: file formats not only get abandoned, but files themselves get marooned on obsolete media.)

It isn’t up to us to say that the photos taken by the first mainstream digital cameras don’t matter. It was up to Apple, and Apple said they don’t.

Then there are the thousands of hours of QuickTime VR production effort expended by museums, educational institutions, photographers, and artists. A staff at Harvard University photographed and produced over 1200 QuickTime VR panoramas shot in and around the pyramids of Giza as part of its Digital Giza project. When Apple abandoned QuickTime VR – and when web browsers evolved beyond plug-ins – those movies became as inaccessible as a pharaoh’s tomb. (Happily, they’re being resurrected in HTML form in an updated website currently in development.)

It’s for these reasons that I created Vintage Viewer. It’s a free macOS app that lets you store, view, and export QuickTime VR movies and QuickTake photos.

I have a small but, to me, significant collection of QuickTake photos and QuickTime VR movies that I shot in the 1990s. I have an elderly PowerBook G4 where I can use the Classic environment to view them and to convert the QuickTake images into JPEG form. (There are a few modern macOS utilities that can convert QuickTake images, too.) But QuickTime VR movies are marooned in the past. I wanted to bring them into the present. I suspected I wasn’t alone.

I wasn’t wrong. As I explored Reddit and other online forums, I saw that others were also interested in these pioneering formats – including people who weren’t even alive when they debuted. (I saw phrases like “my dad had a QuickTake” more than once.) A tool that would enable vintage computing fans to explore and collect these forgotten formats – and that would enable the easy display and conversion of the VR movies created by nonprofits and others – would be my small contribution to the world of digital preservation.

Vintage Viewer converts QuickTake photos as well as QuickTime VR movies, but I don’t want to just convert them. I want to store them in their original formats to be first-class citizens on modern Macs.

You don’t have to create an HTML version of a QTVR movie in order to take it for a spin, or go through a conversion step to view a QuickTake photo. To Vintage Viewer, these formats are as fresh as an iPhone photo.

Rediscovering my vintage images rekindled my interest in these formats. By digging through a cabinet of old CD-ROMs and spending hours exploring the Internet Archive and other websites, I’ve built a collection of nearly 3,000 QuickTime VR movies.

I’ve also reconnected with some pioneering QTVR producers with whom I crossed paths thirty years ago. Photographers Richard Crowest, Doug Cope, and Erik Goetze generously tested Vintage Viewer and supplied some gorgeous movies that are included in a sample library that I include with Vintage Viewer. One of Doug Cope’s movies even has directional sound: as you pan, the soundscape moves between left and right speakers.

All three photographers embraced QuickTime VR in the 1990s and participated in the A Wrinkle in Time VR projects. Those projects, by the way, are all but lost. I did find one of them — the Winter Solstice 1997 edition — buried on a Macworld cover-mount CD-ROM that’s been uploaded to the Internet Archive. I retrieved their movies, but, lacking permission from each of its 80 participants, can’t publish them. My attempts to contact Robert Abbett, the projects’ producer, have been unsuccessful.

As for QuickTake images, I made contact through Reddit with a QuickTake fan in Australia who is only slightly older than the QuickTake cameras he collects. Dean Hucker has what he jokingly calls “a concerning amount of memorabilia surrounding” QuickTake cameras, including a collection of 16 of them. “There are a lot of people out there who do not want dead formats or their devices to disappear and that makes me very happy,” he told me. He’s building an app that can transfer photos from a QuickTake camera via a cable that enables a QuickTake 100 or 150 camera to connect to a USB port. His efforts inspired me to buy a QuickTake 100 on eBay and order the cable.

So I’ve not only rediscovered content made decades ago, I’ve also been inspired to create new content using those forgotten formats. My journey has come full circle – which, when you think about it, seems completely appropriate.

Inside a Modern Hamptons Home With Views in Nearly Every Direction

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Leroy Street Studio designed this Water Mill retreat around a large blended family—and a landscape that changes with every season.

It’s hard to pick a favorite time of year at Peninsula House. Nicknamed for its location on a point overlooking Mecox Bay in Water Mill, New York, the residence was designed by the architecture firm Leroy Street Studio to capitalize on the site’s 270-degree views. An array of indoor and outdoor vantage points capture the ever-changing cinematic interplay of light and water as well as nature’s shifts from season to season.

The owners, a couple with a large blended family, wanted a multigenerational gathering spot that they and their young daughter could share with the husband’s adult children and their spouses as well as friends. From the outset, the clients made it clear they were looking for out-of-the-ordinary architecture that is thoughtfully, creatively integrated into its unique setting. “They really liked the idea of doing something that was countercurrent for the Hamptons,” says Morgan Hare, the Leroy Street founding partner who headed the project.

Adopting a pinwheel-type plan, the striking 14,000-square-foot house features multiple wings radiating from a central core. These spaces vary in scale and height, and some feature deep overhangs, providing shade and amplifying the design’s dynamic horizontality. “The oversailing roofs create a solar brim that helps keep the rooms cool in the summer,” says Alex Witko, a senior associate at Leroy Street who served as the project lead. “They also amplify the views, including from the primary bedroom, where you can just sit and watch the herons on the nearby spit of land that is a protected wildlife preserve.”

Softening the formal rigor, most of the house is topped with lushly planted green roofs and terraces that offer distinct vistas, while the extensively glazed exterior is clad in handmade white brick on the lower levels and dark-stained wood above. The black and white palette is one that Leroy Street, which has designed dozens of waterfront homes in the Hamptons and elsewhere, has rarely used previously. “The dark wood, somewhat counterintuitively, blends into and dematerializes against the vegetation,” Hare says, noting how the house opens on all sides to tree-shaded terraces and courtyards.

Landscape architect Ed Hollander and his firm Hollander Design collaborated on the 1.7-acre property’s outdoor living spaces, while outfitting the gardens with what he calls “maritime habitat” plantings, a mix of native and non-native species “that are adapted to this sunny, salty, breezy environment.”

Everything in the house’s design was conceived around creating a seamless flow—visual and literal—between inside and outside. The main entry consists of a two-story glass-walled gallery that connects the two largest wings, providing views from the front walkway through the house to a rear garden planted with ginkgo trees and the bay beyond. “In the morning, the sun actually comes up through that ginkgo grove and reflects off the entry hall’s glass pendant lights,” notes Hollander. “Then in the evening, the lights become like starlight, these magic things that dance against the trees.”

Inside, the light-and-dark palette continues with the white artisanal brick, expanses of warm plaster, and pale oak and stone offset by inky-hued wood and blackened-steel accents. Throughout, the predominantly new and custom furnishings, overseen by Eleanor Donnelly of Stelle Lomont Rouhani Architects, stick to a sculptural, pared-down aesthetic, complemented by a contemporary art collection that includes works by Teresita Fernández, Sarah Morris, and Adam McEwen. Elevated details like bespoke integrated door pulls and closets with facings in faux-shagreen or mottled resin add subtle character.

The horseshoe-shaped main stairwell is one of few rounded architectural elements in the house. With floating treads and a minimalist oculus at the top, there’s a play of light, shadow, and form that shifts as one moves between floors.

Reaching the home’s dramatic second-story rooftop terrace requires taking a separate, private flight of stairs. A seductive spot for sunsets and stargazing, it features a seating area with a fire bowl surrounded by wispy native grasses and plants.

While a major appeal of this house is all of its intimate spaces for quiet escape, equally important was having versatile areas for gathering in larger groups. That might mean bringing two dozen guests together around the formal dining tables that can be combined and separated as needed or congregating in the various outdoor spaces around the expansive pool area.

Step Inside This Waterfront Hamptons Home

Hare notes that while the firm has designed plenty of residences for extended families, they have typically taken the form of compounds with separate structures. “This is the first one we've done that’s multigenerational and all in one building,” he says. “It was a big part of our mission—and, ultimately, the success of the house.”

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

Operator ALL.Net apologized on Nov 2, but has only just begun to ‘gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today.’

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The sensory overload typically experienced when visiting video game arcades in Japan was eerily absent from some venues this week, reports ITMedia News [machine translation]. It says that a Sega system outage meant there was “no end in sight” for the rows of silent black screens. However, a recent tweet from arcade network operator ALL.net indicates that both Sega and Bandai Namco machines are affected. Moreover, arcade lovers will be pleased to hear that affected arcades will “gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today.”

a friend sent me some pics of their local arcade and LMAO the sega outage is nuts https://t.co/3vSYjCYAIZNovember 3, 2026

ALL.Net, a contraction of Amusement Linkage Live Network, was launched by Sega in 2004 to allow arcade games to look up to the net for auto updates, data logging, implement online play, and power national leaderboards. Sega titles like Initial D The Arcade, Maimai, and CHUNITHM, rely on this network, as do Bandai Namco games like Aiko no Tatsujin, Mario Kart Arcade GP DX, and Gundam Extreme Vs.

In its official apology blog post, dated November 2, ALL.Net / Sega Fave said that it suspended services a day prior after it became aware of its servers being affected by “unauthorized access by a third party.” It reassured customers that their personal info and billing data were not on the compromised server. However, it asked for more time to investigate, run safety checks, and so on, before restoring service.

This morning ALL.Net told customers that Bandia Namco titles “will gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today (online play and card use enabled).” Sega products remain under maintenance, and online services are unavailable. There is currently no estimate for issue resolution, but a news bulletin is promised for November 10 (Tuesday).

Classic offline arcade machines weren’t affected by this outage, of course. Moreover, rival arcade gaming legends like Taito and Konami run their own, separate, arcade networks and authentication systems.

Logitech G502 X Lightspeed Review: No lights, same price

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

This is a great gaming mouse in terms of performance, but it's currently the same price as the prettier G502 X Plus.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Logitech's G502 X Lightspeed has been around for awhile — the mouse was introduced in 2022 alongside the fancier Logitech G502 X Plus (and the wired G502 X), but since it’s receiving a firmware update that upgrades its Hero 25K sensor to a Hero 44K sensor, this seemed like a good time to take a look at it. The G502X Lightspeed is available in black and white colorways and currently retails for $159.99, which is the same price as the G502 X Plus — however, it can (and should) normally be found for less than this.

The Logitech G502 X Lightspeed is a right-handed gaming mouse with an ergonomically contoured shape. It's the same size and shape as the G502 X Plus, with large primary mouse buttons, textured rubber side panels for grip, and a smooth matte chassis with glossy accents. It comes in black and white colorways (our review unit was black) and has no lighting aside from a small indicator light at the front of the mouse to indicate connection and battery life.

Like the G502 X Plus, the G502 X Lightspeed is definitely on the larger side. It measures 5.17 inches (131.4mm) long by 3.12 inches (79.2mm) wide, and is 1.62 inches (41.1mm) high at its tallest point. It's larger than the Razer Basilisk V3 Pro and V4 Pro (5.11 x 2.96 x 1.67 inches / 130 x 75.4 x 42.5mm) — not by a lot, but it definitely feels like a much larger mouse. It weighs about 3.6 ounces (102g), which makes it lighter than the Basilisk V3 Pro, but slightly heavier than the Basilisk V4 Pro.

The G502 X Lightspeed has 11 programmable buttons, and 13 programmable controls if you include scroll up/down. There are the left and right primary mouse buttons, as well as two side buttons along the left edge of the left mouse button. On the left side of the mouse there are the two standard thumb buttons, plus a "sniper" button that's magnetically attached and can be flipped so that it's not in your way. The sniper button can also be "deleted," and Logitech includes a delete cover in the box that makes the button unpressable (and also has the same soft, rubbery texture as the rest of the thumb rest).

The scroll wheel is a 4-way tilt wheel, so it has three programmable "buttons" (left click, right click, and center click), as well as scroll up/down. Above the scroll wheel are two buttons: One is a mechanical scroll wheel switch that switches between notched, tactile scroll and free scroll — that’s not programmable, as it's a physical switch — and the other is a profile cycle button that can be remapped.

On the bottom of the mouse you'll find UHMWPE mouse feet — at the top and bottom, around the sensor, and under the thumb rest. There's a power switch, as well as a magnetically-attached dummy puck that can be swapped out for the charging puck included in the Logitech PowerPlay 2. Under the puck is a slot for storing the mouse's wireless dongle.

In the box, the G502 X Lightspeed comes with a 6-foot (1.8m) USB-C-to-USB-A cable, a USB-A dongle, a USB-C converter, and a sniper button delete cover.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

5.17 x 3.12 x 1.62 inches / 131.4 x 79.2 x 41.1 mm

The Logitech G502 X Lightspeed ships with Logitech's Hero 25K optical sensor, which has a max resolution of 25,600 DPI and a maximum speed of 400 IPS, and can handle up to 40 G's of force. However, both the G502X Lightspeed and the G502 X Plus are receiving a firmware update that will upgrade the Hero 25K optical sensor to a Hero 44K optical sensor, with a maximum resolution of 44,000 DPI and a maximum speed of up to 678 IPS. It's not clear when this upgrade will roll out, however, and I wasn't able to upgrade my review unit in G Hub as of this writing.

Upgrades aside, the G502 X Lightspeed's sensor is very good. It tracks on just about every surface you might possibly use while gaming, and is speedy, sensitive, and very accurate. The mouse supports a polling rate of up to 1,000 Hz, which is still pretty standard for most gamers — however, if you're someone who's convinced that the extra 0.125 milliseconds of lag is the reason for your losses, this is not a mouse with an 8,000 Hz or even a 4,000 Hz polling rate.

The G502 X Lightspeed features Logitech's Lightforce hybrid optical-mechanical switches in its primary mouse buttons, which combine the feel of mechanical switches with the speed and reliability of optical ones. The G502 X Lightspeed's switches do have a nice, tactile click with a crisp sound that's definitely on the louder side. The side buttons also feel and sound good — not quite as tactile as the primary buttons, but with a very crisp click sound.

The mouse features a dual-mode tilt wheel with a mechanical switch that lets you move between a notched, tactile scroll and a smooth, speedy free-scroll. Logitech's implementation of this is different from Razer's — Logitech's is mechanical, while Razer's is software-based. This means the modes are more distinct, but I'm not really sure if that's a good thing. While I liked the free-scroll mode of the G502 X Lightspeed better than I liked the free-scroll mode on Razer's mice (such as the Basilisk V4 Pro and the Naga V3 Pro), the tactile scroll was stiff, clunky, and very loud — with a lot of rattling. The scroll wheel only has two modes, so there's no middle ground — and while I'm fine to use free-scroll for most things, there are definitely games where I need a notched scroll wheel.

The G502 X Lightspeed is configurable via Logitech's universal peripheral software, G Hub. You can use G Hub to change the mouse's DPI settings (DPI steps and DPI shift speed — the DPI switch that occurs when you hit the DPI shift button) and remap keys. You can also use G Hub to update the mouse's firmware, so this is presumably where the Hero 44K sensor upgrade will come through.

The G502 X Lightspeed connects to your PC via 2.4GHz wireless; it can also utilize a wired connection, though the included USB-C cable is rubber and won't get you that smooth, drag-free wired mouse movement you can get with flexible paracord-covered mouse cables. The mouse does not include Bluetooth.

Logitech rates the G502 X Lightspeed at 140 hours of battery life, which is about 10 hours longer than the G502 X Plus (with no lighting). This is pretty impressive, even if there are similar wireless mice that last longer (the recently-launched Razer Basilisk V4 Pro gets up to 200 hours, for example). This should give you at least two weeks before you need to recharge, even with intense use. The G502X Lightspeed is also compatible with the PowerPlay 2, Logitech's wireless charging mousepad (sold separately).

The G502 X Lightspeed is a solid gaming mouse for most gamers — its contoured, ergonomic shape is comfortable, though it does feel bigger than similarly shaped mice, such as the Razer Basilisk V4 Pro. It has enough extra well-placed buttons for the average gamer (though, not enough for the average MMO player), and its 140-hour battery life is higher than average. Plus, assuming it actually gets this firmware update, it will have a competitive sensor — though it's not going to be quite as good as the actual Hero 2 sensor. But this mouse is currently priced at $159.99, which is the same price as the fancier Logitech G502 X Plus — and it's definitely supposed to be the more budget version of the two. Unless you find it at a great sale price, I'd pick up the G502 X Plus or even Razer's Basilisk V3 Pro 35K at a similar price.

After a $2 Billion Exit, Poppi’s Founders Take Aim at Hydration Drinks

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

As an active mother of three boys, 39-year-old Allison Ellsworth struggled to stay hydrated. Usually, she would rip open electrolyte powders and mix them with water, making a mess on the kitchen counter. So her husband Stephen began concocting his own hydrating beverages for her.

One day, Ellsworth looked at him and said: “Oh my god, we’re doing this again, aren’t we?”

Indeed they were. The Ellsworths are the creators of Poppi, the probiotic soda they started in their kitchen and sold last year to PepsiCo for $1.95 billion.

The Ellsworths’ sophomore effort, a hydration drink called Jetty, is making its public debut.

Populus / Studio Gang

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Text description provided by the architects. Studio Gang, the international architecture and urban design firm led by Jeanne Gang, has completed Populus, a 265-room hotel poised to become a new social center for downtown Denver. Marking Studio Gang's first completed project in Colorado and the American Southwest region, Populus's design draws from the growth processes of the state's native Aspen trees (Populus tremuloides) to establish a new architectural icon for Denver. "Denver strikes a unique balance between being a vibrant city and a gateway to some of the country's most awe-inspiring natural landscapes. Our goal was to draw from this distinct urban character and rich ecology to create a building that would help define the skyline of this thriving city," says Jeanne Gang, Founding Partner of Studio Gang.

'We Have Big Plans for 2027': Ripple Engineering Director Teases XRP Ledger Overhaul

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Ripple's director of engineering Viijay Khanna directly confirmed that the company is readying 'big plans' for XRP Ledger in 2027. The remark came amid the debates around the blockchain's speed, when critics put the XRP network's stats (around 1,500 transactions per second) against the average daily load of Visa, labeling the XRPL "an outdated copy of legacy payment systems".

"We have big plans for performance improvements lined up for 2027", said Khanna, effectively ending speculations.

Ripple's engineering department is aiming to exit the race for beautiful numbers behind the TPS metric. The logic is that the basic Layer-1 does not need to digest millions of small transactions directly. Retail microtraffic then goes to the second layer like Dassie (Interledger). The task of the main layer thus becomes maintaining large funds of banks and institutions.

The first step to this kind of independence from "heavy blockchain metal" was made this month with the fresh release of xrpld 3.3.0 which cut the appetite of nodes to RAM by 13-23%.

Technical audits show that the issue was not in simple code flaws, but the caprices of the system's allocator: the software was literally withholding the server's resources after the cache emptying. Bug fix therefore allowed validators to radically decrease infrastructure costs.

For further improvement of the current operational environment on November 4, the network got a fixCleanup package.

Khanna's short commentary is not an attempt to snuff out critics, but a marker of an ecosystem's overhaul for the needs of commercial banks. Ripple's president Monica Long has already announced a plan to relay the big chunk of the company's clients' payments directly on XRP Ledger. Cross-border remittances will be tied to new lending infrastructure as well.

To make sure the network will not flood from institutional funds, XRP Ledger Foundation already presented a 2027 roadmap with these as main highlights:

The overhaul of XRP Ledger is a preventive hit on the network's future overload. Ripple ecosystem builders rewrite the basis, using AI teams for strict testing and code optimization. XRP Ledger is being readied for the moment when transactions with RLUSD stablecoin, tokenized stocks trading, and interbank lending become a routine and not just pilot projects.

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Arsenal beat Liverpool 2-1 in the WSL on Sunday at the West Lancashire College Stadium, Skelmersdale. Every goal came before the interval. Liverpool have now gone six matches without a win in all competitions.

The visitors needed only nine minutes to go ahead. Emily Fox supplied the assist and Alessia Russo finished.

Liverpool’s reply arrived in the 15th minute. Natalia Ramos Álvarez set up Beata Olsson to level the scores.

The decisive goal came on the stroke of half-time. Lisa Baum provided the pass and Russo scored again in the 45th minute. Nobody found the net after the break, and the only booking of the afternoon went to Caitlin Foord for a foul in the 80th minute.

Russo finished with a match rating of 9.03, the highest on the pitch, from her 3 shots. Fox, rated 7.83, made 4 key passes. Olsson was Liverpool’s best performer at 7.48.

Arsenal had 53% of the ball and outshot their hosts 19 to 14, with 6 efforts on target against 4. Their expected goals figure of 2.05 compared with 1.43 for Liverpool, who still won more corners, 6 to 4. The home side were caught offside 3 times. Arsenal never were.

Liverpool made 4 saves to Arsenal’s 3, and both teams committed 6 fouls.

The head-to-head keeps tilting towards the Londoners. This was Arsenal’s fourth straight win over Liverpool, whose last victory in the fixture was a 1-0 success in the FA Women’s Cup on 9 March 2025. The most recent league meeting, on 16 May 2026, ended in a 3-1 win for Arsenal with Liverpool at home.

Liverpool are 11th with 8 points from 9 games. Birmingham City and Crystal Palace are two points behind them on 6, and each has a match in hand.

Both sides play again on Wednesday 11 November. Liverpool host Aston Villa in the WSL Cup, having lost 2-1 to them during this winless run, while Arsenal travel to Barcelona in the Champions League.

Parker’s words prove he has so much more to offer than his predecessor | West Ham News

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

West Ham fans may have voiced their fury as the side slid one place further down the table after a poor run of results, but at least in Scott Parker the Hammers have a manager who understands how it really, really feels to have to witness that dross.

Unlike his predecessor Nuno Espírito Santo, Parker seems to have the ability to connect with the fan base and offer real empathy – along with hope that he will at least, work his nuts off to see a turnaround.

Nuno Espirito Santo was reduced to the same platitudes, the same apologies, but with the sense that it was just for the cameras and that he’d shrug his shoulders as soon as the interview was over, leaving someone else to fix the chaos in the dressing room and on the training pitch. West Ham’s training videos usually showed an assistant taking training, with Nuno either passive or absent entirely.

Even after a year Nuno struggled to convince fans that his ‘concern’ was heartfelt.

Parker has already surpassed his predecessor in terms of being ‘present’. Both his pronouncements and his actions – such as his first-game, half time team talk demonstrate his ability to wear his heart on his sleeve.

His words reported yesterday by footballleagueworld.co.uk do suggest, unlike his predecessor, that he’s genuinely hurting, along with the rest of us:

“Sounding somewhat hoarse after his team’s 2-1 defeat at Blackburn, Scott Parker spoke after the matchabout his disappointment at the result. Parker said that he’d talked about his team’s need to get a result from this match, even if it wasn’t playing the sort of football that he want his team to play.

He had sympathy for West Ham supporters, saying that he “wanted to get three points” for them, adding that, “What I can say is that I’m fully committed here, and I understand the pain, I understand where the fan base are.”

Parker added that he understands that the unhappiness of fans isn’t entirely a response to recent performances alone, saying that, “That’s not just this season, it’s come over a little bit of time.”

And he admitted that he was also hurting from this result: “I think everyone’s hurting here, and tonight I’m hurting because I understand that the only way we can change things is winning”

And it matters. In these dark days for the football club, mid table in the second division, leadership is what will unite team and supporters to operate together and hopefully pull the club out of its slide. Leadership doesn’t always yell, ( he’s done that too), sometimes it merely shows understanding and empathy whilst demanding better.

The end of an era as Linux 7.5 purges the last 30-year-old Intel MMX optimizations from Pentium age

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

MMX was introduced in 1997 and marketed as a MultiMedia eXtension for Intel’s processor architecture in shiny new PCs with CD-ROMs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Linux developers are preparing to make Intel’s ancient MultiMedia eXtensions an irrelevance. Intel introduced MMX in the Pentium era, but a patch expected to merge into mainline Linux 7.5 will remove the last remaining optimized software from the open-source OS. Expected to be released in 2027, the new kernel will wipe MMX-optimized code from its software RAID XOR code, explains Phoronix.

Admittedly, there wasn’t much left in Linux in terms of MMX optimizations. But few folks will be running the most modern kernels on Pentium MMX and Pentium II chips in 2026. And most other MMX-optimized code disappeared from the OS half a decade ago, when some MMX-optimized memory management code was removed. Note that, if you are a determined late-90s Pentium devotee, you can still use software RAID, but it will lack the MMX fast path.

This is a classic case of Linux code housekeeping. With this change, the kernel will lose over 800 lines of code and replace them with just 12.

Intel first introduced MMX Pentium chips in January 1997. Though I vaguely remember these processors being marketed as being enhanced by ‘MultiMedia eXtensions,’ Wikipedia insists that “MMX is officially a meaningless initialism[6] trademarked by Intel.”

In a subsequent trademark battle with longtime foe AMD, a document produced in court indicated that MMX stood for ‘Matrix Math Extensions.’ AMD relied on this to sidestep the trademark, as an initialism cannot be trademarked, according to the encyclopedic source.

Eventually, all rival x86 processors managed to integrate MMX instructions – so as not to be pushed into the shade during the multimedia boom. A surprisingly large number of non-Intel chips ended up offering MMX, too. Along with Pentium MMX chips, AMD, Centaur, Cyrix, Rise, and Transmeta eventually offered MMX CPUs.

After MMX's marketing success, AMD pioneered 3DNow! instructions, and about a year later, Intel fired back with SSE and SSE2.

Anthropic buys $3.4bn of gas generators for its data centres, FT reports

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The AI company took over 1.7 gigawatts of Caterpillar generator orders from San Antonio firm OnePWR Solutions in September.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei arrives near the White House for a meeting with President Donald Trump, Washington, DC, 29 September 2026

The deal covers 1.7 gigawatts of large generators, the FT said, citing one person who knows about it. Anthropic signed it in late September with OnePWR Solutions, a power supplier in San Antonio, Texas. TNW has not independently confirmed the deal.

OnePWR had ordered the generators from Caterpillar about two years ago but needed money to complete the purchase, the FT reported. Anthropic first planned to buy power from OnePWR. Instead, it took over the orders, plus the work needed to install the machines.

Anthropic did not buy OnePWR itself, and none of its staff moved to Anthropic, according to the FT. It is not known where the generators will be used.

Both companies declined to comment to the FT. They also did not immediately respond to Bloomberg, which followed up the report.

The deal comes as Anthropic prepares for its stock market listing. Its amended IPO filing last week showed $895bn in commitments, almost all for computing power and infrastructure.

Other AI firms have also used gas to get data centres running fast. Elon Musk’s xAI faces a lawsuit over gas turbines at its Memphis site.

Dylan Skolnick Returning as Co-Director of the Cinema Arts Centre Following Protests and Boycotts

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Dylan Skolnick is returning as Director of the Cinema Arts Centre following protests and boycotts over his firing.

In a post announcing Skolnick’s reinstatement, the Long Island cultural institution said that its board of directors had sat down for a series of meetings with an independent mediator. “Those discussions have resulted in an agreement to move forward together, and we are pleased to welcome Dylan back to the Cinema,” the announcement read.

In September, Skolnick was ousted by the CAC board of directors for “serious misconduct.” In justifying the move, the organization slammed Skolnick as a “dysfunctional” and “disloyal” manager whose freelance work as a film buyer for other indie cinemas was a conflict of interest. Skolnick and his supporters countered that the CAC board was experiencing a “hostile takeover” and said that his moonlighting as a film buyer was common practice in the business. Skolnick said he had informed an earlier iteration of the board of his work, but conceded that the current leadership was unaware of his side gig. Related Stories How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Used Real-World Sound to Make Its Monsters More Terrifying ‘Zi’ Review: Kogonada Rediscovers His Voice with a Microbudget Hong Kong Experiment Starring Michelle Mao and Haley Lu Richardson

Skolnick had deep ties to the institution, which his parents had founded in Huntington, New York more than 50 years ago, as well as strong connections to the wider filmmaking world. Major figures such as Edie Falco, Kent Jones, and Isabella Rossellini leapt to his support, while donations to the CAC dried up, and box office receipts cratered, IndieWire reported last month.

The CAC said that as part of Skolnick’s reinstatement, a new co-director will be hired in the coming months to help lead the organization and its board will invite several new members, “including individuals with backgrounds and experience in the film world.” Skolnick and his fellow co-director and and the CAC board will “undergo additional training focused on effective governance, leadership, accountability and fostering a positive workplace culture.”

In a statement, the CAC leadership acknowledged the fissures created by Skolnick’s ouster, as well as the need to repair relationships and address the non-profit’s financial challenges.

“We want to extend our deepest gratitude to everyone who spoke up, showed their support, and stood by the Cinema during one of the most challenging periods in its history,” the theater said in a statement. “Your voices made a difference, and your continued commitment to this institution means more than we can express.”
